Perhaps the broader project of establishing the affine kernel integral representation under the standard decision-theoretic package could still survive.

By this, we mean that the above counterexample only illustrated the invalidity of the conclusions of both the one-sided primal and one-sided dual theorems, thereby undermining the validity of the current proof strategy of the main theorem, as it is based on them. However, it is not actually a counterexample to the main theorem itself. Nevertheless, given the length and intricacy of the following proofs even under additional strengthened conditions, it seems such a proof would be far more difficult than originally conceived.

4.3
Analytical Preliminaries

This subsection presents standard techniques and results from real and functional analysis. They are included only to keep the paper self-contained.
We begin this review by presenting the derivations and properties of bump and mollifier functions, as they will be invoked repeatedly. We then proceed to Hahn–Banach–type and Riesz–Markov–type machinery. Interested readers may consult the appendix.
Lemma
3
is a standard uniqueness fact for locally finite Borel measures tested against smooth compactly supported functions; cf.
[
26
,
10
,
32
]
.
Lemmas
4
–
6
are standard Hahn–Banach extension/separation results; cf.
[
15
,
5
]
.
Lemma
7
is a specialization of the Riesz representation theorem on
C([0,1])
; cf.
[
48
,
26
]
.

Lemma 1

(Smooth bump, standard mollifier, and interval cutoffs)
.

Define
\rho:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
by

\rho(s):=\begin{cases}e^{-1/s},&s>0,\\
0,&s\leq 0.\end{cases}

Then
\rho\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
\rho^{(k)}(0)=0
for every
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

Set
\varphi(t):=\rho(1-t^{2})
,
A:=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\varphi(t)\,dt
, and
\psi:=\varphi/A
. Then
\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
,
\psi\geq 0
,
\supp(\psi)=[-1,1]
, and
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi(t)\,dt=1
.

For
\varepsilon>0
, define
\psi_{\varepsilon}(t):=\varepsilon^{-1}\psi(t/\varepsilon)
for
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Then
\psi_{\varepsilon}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
,
\psi_{\varepsilon}\geq 0
,
\supp(\psi_{\varepsilon})\subset[-\varepsilon,\varepsilon]
, and
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi_{\varepsilon}(t)\,dt=1
.

If
0<a<b<1
and
0<\varepsilon<\min\{a,1-b\}
, define

\eta_{a,b,\varepsilon}(u):=\int_{a}^{b}\psi_{\varepsilon}(u-s)\,ds\qquad(u\in\mathbb{R}).

(6)

Then
\eta_{a,b,\varepsilon}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(0,1)
, and

0\leq\eta_{a,b,\varepsilon}(u)\leq 1\qquad\forall\,u\in\mathbb{R},

(7)

\supp(\eta_{a,b,\varepsilon})\subset[a-\varepsilon,b+\varepsilon]\subset(0,1),

(8)

\eta_{a,b,\varepsilon}\equiv 1\qquad\text{on }[a+\varepsilon,b-\varepsilon].

(9)

Proof.
See the proof in Appendix
A

Lemma 2

(Approximation of interval indicators by interval cutoffs)
.

Fix
0<a<b<1
, and set

\delta:=\min\{a,\ 1-b,\ (b-a)/8\}>0,\qquad\varepsilon_{n}:=\delta/n,\qquad\chi_{n}:=\eta_{a+2\varepsilon_{n},\ b-2\varepsilon_{n},\ \varepsilon_{n}}\qquad(n\in\mathbb{N}).

Then
\chi_{n}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(0,1)
for every
n
, and

0\leq\chi_{n}\leq\mathbf{1}_{(a,b)}\qquad\text{on }(0,1)\qquad(n\in\mathbb{N}),

(10)

\chi_{n}(u)\longrightarrow\mathbf{1}_{(a,b)}(u)\qquad\forall\,u\in(0,1).

(11)

Proof.
See the proof in Appendix
A

Lemma 3

(Determination of locally finite Borel measures by
C_{c}^{\infty}(0,1)
)
.

Let
\alpha
and
\beta
be nonnegative Borel measures on
(0,1)
. Assume that

\alpha(K)<\infty,\qquad\beta(K)<\infty\qquad\text{for every compact set }K\Subset(0,1).

Let
\lambda\in[0,\infty)
. If

\int_{(0,1)}\eta(u)\,\alpha(du)=\lambda\int_{(0,1)}\eta(u)\,\beta(du)\qquad\forall\,\eta\in C_{c}^{\infty}(0,1),

then
\alpha=\lambda\beta
as Borel measures on
(0,1)
.

Proof.
See the proof in Appendix
A

Lemma 4

(One-dimensional dominated extension for a sublinear functional)
.

Let
X
be a real vector space and let
p:X\to\mathbb{R}
be sublinear:

p(\lambda x)=\lambda p(x)\quad(\lambda\geq 0,\ x\in X),\qquad p(x+y)\leq p(x)+p(y)\quad(x,y\in X).

Let
Y\subset X
be a linear subspace and let
F_{0}:Y\to\mathbb{R}
be linear with

F_{0}(y)\leq p(y)\quad(\forall y\in Y).

Let
x_{0}\in X\setminus Y
and set
Y_{1}:=Y\oplus\mathbb{R}x_{0}
. Define

\alpha:=\sup_{y\in Y}\bigl(F_{0}(y)-p(y-x_{0})\bigr),\qquad\beta:=\inf_{y\in Y}\bigl(p(y+x_{0})-F_{0}(y)\bigr).

Then
\alpha\leq\beta
, and for each
t\in[\alpha,\beta]
the map
F_{t}:Y_{1}\to\mathbb{R}
given by

F_{t}(y+sx_{0}):=F_{0}(y)+st\qquad(y\in Y,\ s\in\mathbb{R})

is a well-defined linear functional such that
F_{t}|_{Y}=F_{0}
and

F_{t}(z)\leq p(z)\quad\text{for all }z\in Y_{1}.

Proof.
See the proof in Appendix
A

Lemma 5

(Hahn–Banach extension dominated by sublinear functional)
.

Let
X
be a real vector space and let
p:X\to\mathbb{R}
be sublinear:

p(\lambda x)=\lambda p(x)\quad(\lambda\geq 0,\ x\in X),\qquad p(x+y)\leq p(x)+p(y)\quad(x,y\in X).

Let
Y\subset X
be a linear subspace and let
F_{0}:Y\to\mathbb{R}
be linear with

F_{0}(y)\leq p(y)\quad(\forall y\in Y).

Then there exists a linear functional
F:X\to\mathbb{R}
such that

F|_{Y}=F_{0},\qquad F(x)\leq p(x)\quad(\forall x\in X).

Proof.
See the proof in Appendix
A

Lemma 6

(Hahn–Banach separation for convex sets)
.

Let
(X,|\cdot|)
be a real normed linear space. For arbitrary nonempty convex sets
A,B\subset X
with
A\cap B=\varnothing
and
\operatorname{int}A\neq\varnothing
, there exist a nonzero continuous linear functional
F:X\to\mathbb{R}
and a real number
\gamma
such that

F(a)\geq\gamma\geq F(b)\quad\text{for all }a\in A,\ b\in B.

Proof.
See the proof in Appendix
A

Lemma 7

(Positive Linear Functional Representation on
C([0,1])
(right–continuous version))
.

Let
X:=C([0,1])
with the supremum norm
\|f\|_{\infty}:=\sup_{x\in[0,1]}|f(x)|
.
Let
L:X\to\mathbb{R}
be a
continuous
linear functional such that

f\geq 0\ \text{on }[0,1]\quad\Longrightarrow\quad L(f)\geq 0.

Define for
t\in[0,1)
:

\mathcal{L}_{t}:=\bigl\{\phi\in X:\ 0\leq\phi\leq 1,\ \phi(x)=0\ \text{for all }x\in[t,1]\bigr\},\qquad\alpha(t):=\sup\{\,L(\phi):\ \phi\in\mathcal{L}_{t}\,\}.

Define at
t=1
:

\mathcal{L}_{1}:=\{\phi\in X:\ 0\leq\phi\leq 1\},\qquad\alpha(1):=\sup\{\,L(\phi):\ \phi\in\mathcal{L}_{1}\,\}.

Then
\alpha:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
is increasing, the Riemann–Stieltjes integral
\int_{0}^{1}f\,d\alpha
exists for every
f\in X
, and

L(f)=\int_{0}^{1}f(x)\,d\alpha(x)\qquad\forall f\in X.

Moreover, define the
right–continuous modification

\alpha^{\mathrm{rc}}(0):=\alpha(0),\qquad\alpha^{\mathrm{rc}}(t):=\alpha(t+)\ (t\in(0,1)),\qquad\alpha^{\mathrm{rc}}(1):=\alpha(1),

(12)

where
\alpha(t+):=\lim_{h\downarrow 0}\alpha(t+h)
.
Then
\alpha^{\mathrm{rc}}
is increasing and right–continuous on
(0,1)
, the integral
\int_{0}^{1}f\,d\alpha^{\mathrm{rc}}
exists for every

f\in X
, and

L(f)=\int_{0}^{1}f\,d\alpha^{\mathrm{rc}}\qquad\forall f\in X.

(In particular, after replacing
\alpha
by
\alpha^{\mathrm{rc}}
one may assume
\alpha
is right–continuous on
(0,1)
.)

Proof.
See the proof in Appendix
A

Proposition 1

(Affine continuous representation on the Lorenz domain (Herstein–Milnor construction))
.

Let
X:=C([0,1])
with
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
, and let

\mathcal{L}:=\{L\in X:\ L(0)=0,\ L(1)=1,\ L\ \text{is nondecreasing and convex}\}.

Let
\succsim
be a binary relation on
\mathcal{L}
satisfying
Axioms
1
,
5
, and
4
. Moreover, it satisfies the
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
version of closed-graph continuity:

The graph

\{(L_{1},L_{2})\in\mathcal{L}\times\mathcal{L}:\ L_{1}\succsim L_{2}\}

is closed in
\mathcal{L}\times\mathcal{L}
, where
\mathcal{L}
carries the topology induced by

(C([0,1]),\|\cdot\|_{\infty})
.
Then there exists a nonconstant function
U:\mathcal{L}\to\mathbb{R}
such that:

(i)

For all
L_{1},L_{2}\in\mathcal{L}
,

L_{1}\succsim L_{2}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad U(L_{1})\geq U(L_{2}).

(ii)

For all
L_{1},L_{2}\in\mathcal{L}
and all
\alpha\in[0,1]
,

U\bigl((1-\alpha)L_{1}+\alpha L_{2}\bigr)=(1-\alpha)U(L_{1})+\alpha U(L_{2}).

(iii)

U
is continuous on
\mathcal{L}
with respect to
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
.

(iv)

If
V:\mathcal{L}\to\mathbb{R}
also satisfies
(i)
–
(ii)
, then there exist
a>0
and
b\in\mathbb{R}
such that

V=aU+b\quad\text{on }\mathcal{L}.

Moreover, for any fixed
L^{1}\succ L^{0}
one may normalize
U(L^{1})=1
and
U(L^{0})=0
.

Proof.
See the proof in Appendix
A

4.4
Representation Theorems for Lorenz Curves: Dyadic Discretization and One-sided Representation

Before formally delivering the main results of this paper, we would like to provide some metaphorical explanation for the ideas behind our new proof structures for the first and second representation theorems for Lorenz ordering. Lorenz curves are inhabitants of an infinite-dimensional function space. However, many arguments that are standard in finite-dimensional settings fail in the infinite-dimensional case. For example, in finite dimensions it is possible to establish the existence of a linear functional representation by means of open-set separation. In the full infinite-dimensional space, the analogous translation set would have empty interior under the
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
norm, and the same interior based separation argument would therefore fail directly.

As we have seen, the absence of such an implicit assumption leads to counterexamples, which imply that the primal and dual theorems in their original form cannot be true. Strictly speaking, this is not directly fatal, given that the final goal is to derive the metric representation of Egalitarianism from the qualitative ordering of curves. However, these two theorems serve as key footholds that we need to leverage in the original project. As we attempt to climb the otherwise smooth mountain of the representation theorem, in the absence of strengthened boundary conditions, we have no auxiliary intermediate representations to rely on. Therefore, if a similar representation result were still to hold, it seems that it would necessarily require more delicate techniques and would have to differ in a fundamental way from relatively common constructions in analysis or measure theory, since the existence of singularities may preclude all the mainstream comparable structures.

On the other hand, it is possible to circumvent these obstructions by directly imposing regularity conditions on the kernel functions themselves. However, such conditions are not intrinsic to the general framework of Lorenz curves. In light of the observation regarding the difficulty of deriving a representation theorem from the current axiomatic system, and drawing on earlier studies on equivalent formulations of continuity, here we try to hit a balance between assuming too much and too little by introducing the following new axiom which is constructed using the intrinsic objects from Lorenz domain:

Axiom 7

(Archimedean on the Lorenz domain)
.

Let
E:[0,1]\to[0,1]
be the equality curve
E(u):=u
. Let

\mathcal{K}\in\{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}\}
be the relevant Lorenz domain.
There exist an anchor
B\in\mathcal{K}
, a constant
C>0
, and a radius
\delta>0
such that

E\succ B,

Then for every
L\in\mathcal{K}
with

0<\|E-L\|_{\infty}<\delta
, there exists
t_{L}\in[0,1]
satisfying

t_{L}\leq C\|E-L\|_{\infty}\qquad\text{and}\qquad L\succsim(1-t_{L})E+t_{L}B.

It is helpful to describe the architecture of the proofs and their places among current literature. The arguments from theorem
5
to the main theorem
8
appear to be new in this setting. Of course, their novelty is not in introducing new general analytical machinery,
but in combining standard tools from Functional/Convex analysis, measure theory in a way tailored to the Lorenz domain.

More precisely, the paper builds the characterization result through three methodological layers.
The next Theorem
5
is a rather conventional finite-dimensional approximation argument. The continuous Lorenz curve is embedded into a discretized dyadic polygonal subspace in which interior geometry is restored so that classical convex separation becomes available again. The resulting finite-dimensional linear functional is then rewritten as a step-kernel Stieltjes representation by Abel summation.

Then theorems
6
and
7
would be built on that discrete representation as the continuum limit of the previous discretized representation. Under Axiom
7
, the dyadic kernels satisfy uniform mass bounds, which allows one to pass to the limit by diagonal extraction and the Riesz representation theorem. In the dual case, one first conjugates the preorder by the involution operation
\mathcal{S}
, which transforms dual mixtures into ordinary mixtures, and then applies the same discretization-and-limit machinery to the transported problem. Finally, the main Theorem
8
is different in character. Indeed, the main proof is organized around a parametrized functional construction, with various analytical and measure-theoretic arguments interwoven throughout to bridge the gaps and connect the different steps. The purpose of this construction is to overcome pointwise discontinuities and to impose a global structural rigidity constraint governing the primal and dual measures. Ultimately, these analytical structures that we ourselves constructed will impose a rigidity that forces the kernels
p
and
q
to be affine.

The discrete layer is mainly borrowed from convex analysis and piecewise-affine approximation theory, see
[
45
,
49
,
15
,
36
,
5
,
51
,
12
,
18
]
. The limiting arguments related to standard positive-functional representation and measure-theoretic compactness arguments, see, e.g,
[
48
,
47
,
7
,
26
,
10
,
21
,
9
,
46
,
56
,
5
]
. For the dual side, also see the existing economics literature
[
64
,
52
,
2
,
37
]
. For the general background about variational and
BV
methods arguments that appeared in the main rigidity theorem, check
[
44
,
6
,
13
,
32
,
26
,
10
,
23
]
.

Theorem 5

(Polygonal Discretization of Lorenz–Stieltjes Curve Representation)
.

Let

\mathcal{K}\in\{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}\}\subset C([0,1]).

Let
\succsim
be a complete preorder on
\mathcal{K}
, and let

U:\mathcal{K}\to\mathbb{R}

be an affine
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
–continuous, nonconstant representation of
\succsim
.
Assume
weak dominance
:

G_{1}(u)\geq G_{2}(u)\ \forall u\in[0,1]\quad\Longrightarrow\quad G_{1}\succsim G_{2}.

For each
m\in\mathbb{N}
set

n_{m}:=2^{m},\qquad t_{m,k}:=\frac{k}{n_{m}}\qquad(k=0,\dots,n_{m}),

and define the polygonal discretization subspace

X_{m}:=\Bigl\{g\in C([0,1]):g\text{ is affine on each }[t_{m,k-1},t_{m,k}]\ (k=1,\dots,n_{m})\Bigr\},\qquad\mathcal{K}_{m}:=\mathcal{K}\cap X_{m}.

Then there exists
m_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for every
m\geq m_{0}
there exist

•

a right–continuous step function
p_{m}:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
that is constant on each
[t_{m,k-1},t_{m,k})
,
is nonincreasing, and satisfies
p_{m}(1)=0
;

•

a finite nonnegative Borel measure
\nu_{m}
supported on
\{t_{m,1},\dots,t_{m,n_{m}-1}\}
,

such that for every
G,H\in\mathcal{K}_{m}
,

U(G)-U(H)=\int_{0}^{1}p_{m}(u)\,d\bigl(G-H\bigr)(u),

(13)

where, for every
g\in X_{m}
,

\int_{0}^{1}p_{m}(u)\,dg(u):=\sum_{k=1}^{n_{m}}p(m,k)\bigl(g(t_{m,k})-g(t_{m,k-1})\bigr),\qquad p_{m}|_{[t_{m,k-1},t_{m,k})}\equiv p(m,k),\ \ p(m,n_{m})=0.

Moreover, defining the grid
drops

\alpha(m,k):=p(m,k)-p(m,k+1)\qquad(k=1,\dots,n_{m}-1),

one may take

\nu_{m}=\sum_{k=1}^{n_{m}-1}\alpha(m,k)\,\delta_{t_{m,k}},

and the discrete summation-by-parts identity holds:

\int_{0}^{1}p_{m}\,d(G-H)=\int_{[0,1]}\bigl(G(u)-H(u)\bigr)\,\nu_{m}(du)=\sum_{k=1}^{n_{m}-1}\bigl(G(t_{m,k})-H(t_{m,k})\bigr)\,\nu_{m}(\{t_{m,k}\})

(14)

for all
G,H\in\mathcal{K}_{m}
.
In particular,

\nu_{m}\geq 0,\qquad\nu_{m}([0,1])=p(m,1)\geq 0.

Proof.

By proposition
1
, we have an affine continuous representation
U
that represents the complete preorder on the Lorenz Domain, our goal is to constructively prove the existence of its integral representation form when the domain of
U
is restricted to finite-dimensional embedding space of discretized Lorenz curves. The proof would be invoked twice in the future as it applies to both Lorenz representation theorems under independence and Dual independence assumptions, respectively.

Step 1: Dyadic grids, Polygonal subspaces, and Cone Interior.

Let
X:=C([0,1])
with
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
.

For each
m\in\mathbb{N}
set

n_{m}:=2^{m},\qquad t_{m,k}:=\frac{k}{n_{m}}\qquad(k=0,1,\dots,n_{m}).

Define:

X_{m}:=\Bigl\{g\in C([0,1]):g\text{ is affine on each }[t_{m,k-1},t_{m,k}]\ (k=1,\dots,n_{m})\Bigr\},

\mathcal{L}_{m}:=\mathcal{L}\cap X_{m},\qquad\mathcal{L}_{m}^{\uparrow}:=\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}\cap X_{m}.\qquad\mathcal{K}_{m}:=\mathcal{K}\cap X_{m}.

Define the polygonal interpolation operator
P_{m}:X\to X_{m}
by prescribing nodal values

(P_{m}f)(t_{m,k})=f(t_{m,k})\qquad(k=0,1,\dots,n_{m}),

and letting
P_{m}f
be affine on each
[t_{m,k-1},t_{m,k}]
.
Then
P_{m}f\to f
uniformly for every
f\in X
.

Now fix
L\in\mathcal{K}
. On each
[t_{m,k-1},t_{m,k}]
the slope of
P_{m}L
equals the secant slope

s_{m,k}:=\frac{L(t_{m,k})-L(t_{m,k-1})}{t_{m,k}-t_{m,k-1}}=n_{m}\bigl(L(t_{m,k})-L(t_{m,k-1})\bigr).

Convexity of
L
implies that
(s_{m,k})_{k=1}^{n_{m}}
is nondecreasing.
Because
L
is increasing, we have
s_{m,k}\geq 0
for all
k
; if
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}
, then in fact

s_{m,k}>0
for all
k
.
Also

P_{m}L(0)=L(0)=0,\qquad P_{m}L(1)=L(1)=1.

Hence

P_{m}L\in\mathcal{K}_{m}.

Anchoring pair and induced finite-dimensional utility.

Choose
L^{+},L^{-}\in\mathcal{K}
with
U(L^{+})>U(L^{-})
and set

\eta:=\frac{U(L^{+})-U(L^{-})}{4}>0.

By continuity of
U
there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that

\|L-L^{+}\|_{\infty}<\varepsilon\Longrightarrow|U(L)-U(L^{+})|<\eta,\qquad\|L-L^{-}\|_{\infty}<\varepsilon\Longrightarrow|U(L)-U(L^{-})|<\eta.

Choose
m_{0}
such that for all
m\geq m_{0}
,

\|P_{m}L^{+}-L^{+}\|_{\infty}<\varepsilon,\qquad\|P_{m}L^{-}-L^{-}\|_{\infty}<\varepsilon.

Then for all
m\geq m_{0}
,

U(P_{m}L^{+})\geq U(L^{+})-\eta>U(L^{-})+\eta\geq U(P_{m}L^{-}).

Fix
m\geq m_{0}
and denote
n:=n_{m},\quad t_{k}:=t_{m,k}\quad(k=0,1,\dots,n)
, set

L_{m}^{+}:=P_{m}L^{+},\qquad L_{m}^{-}:=P_{m}L^{-},\qquad L_{m}^{0}:=\frac{L_{m}^{+}+L_{m}^{-}}{2}\in\mathcal{K}_{m},

and define the centered affine utility on
\mathcal{K}_{m}
by

U_{m}(L):=U(L)-U(L_{m}^{0})\qquad(L\in\mathcal{K}_{m}).

Then
U_{m}
is affine on
\mathcal{K}_{m}
, represents
\succsim
on
\mathcal{K}_{m}
, and satisfies

U_{m}(L_{m}^{0})=0,\qquad U_{m}(L_{m}^{+})>0,\qquad U_{m}(L_{m}^{-})<0.

Let

X_{m}^{0}:=\{h\in X_{m}:\ h(0)=h(1)=0\},

with
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
. Each
h\in X_{m}^{0}
is determined by
(h(t_{1}),\dots,h(t_{n-1}))\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
.

For
j=1,\dots,n-1
define
H_{m,j}\in X_{m}^{0}
by

H_{m,j}(t_{j})=1,\qquad H_{m,j}(t_{k})=0\ (k\neq j),\qquad H_{m,j}\ \text{affine on each }[t_{k-1},t_{k}].

Then, for every
h\in X_{m}^{0}
,

h=\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}h(t_{j})\,H_{m,j}.

(15)

Indeed both sides lie in
X_{m}^{0}
and coincide at all nodes
t_{k}
, hence coincide on each
[t_{k-1},t_{k}]
.

Note that for each
m
and each
g\in X_{m}
,

\|g\|_{\infty}=\max_{0\leq k\leq n_{m}}|g(t_{m,k})|.

Indeed, on each interval
[t_{m,k-1},t_{m,k}]
the function
g
is affine, hence its maximum and minimum are attained at endpoints. In particular, for
h\in X_{m}^{0}
,

\|h\|_{\infty}=\max_{1\leq k\leq n-1}|h(t_{k})|

(16)

because
h(t_{0})=h(t_{n})=0
. Hence
\|H_{m,j}\|_{\infty}=1
for each
j
.

Define the cone

C_{m}:=\{h\in X_{m}^{0}:\ h(u)\geq 0\ \forall u\in[0,1]\},\qquad C_{m}^{\circ}:=\{h\in X_{m}^{0}:\ h(t_{k})>0\ \forall k=1,\dots,n-1\}.

Then

C_{m}^{\circ}=\operatorname{int}_{X_{m}^{0}}(C_{m}).

(17)

(\subset)
Let
h\in C_{m}^{\circ}
and set
m(h):=\min_{1\leq k\leq n-1}h(t_{k})>0
.
If
\|g-h\|_{\infty}<m(h)
then by (
16
),

g(t_{k})\geq h(t_{k})-\|g-h\|_{\infty}>0\qquad(k=1,\dots,n-1).

By affinity of
g
on each
[t_{k-1},t_{k}]
, positivity at endpoints implies
g\geq 0
pointwise, so
g\in C_{m}
.
Thus
B(h,m(h))\subset C_{m}
and
h\in\operatorname{int}_{X_{m}^{0}}(C_{m})
.

(\supset)
Let
h\in C_{m}
with
h(t_{j})=0
for some
j\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
.
For any
\delta>0
set
g:=h-\delta H_{m,j}
. Then

\|g-h\|_{\infty}=\delta\|H_{m,j}\|_{\infty}=\delta,\qquad g(t_{j})=-\delta<0,

so
g\notin C_{m}
. Hence no open ball around
h
is contained in
C_{m}
, i.e.
h\notin\operatorname{int}_{X_{m}^{0}}(C_{m})
.
Therefore
\operatorname{int}_{X_{m}^{0}}(C_{m})\subset C_{m}^{\circ}
.

Step 2: Translation of Disjoint Open Convex Sets.

Let

\mathcal{D}_{m}:=\{d(L):=L-L_{m}^{0}:\ L\in\mathcal{K}_{m}\}\subset X_{m}^{0},\qquad U_{m}^{0}(d(L)):=U_{m}(L).

Then
\mathcal{D}_{m}
is convex and
U_{m}^{0}
is affine on
\mathcal{D}_{m}
. Define convex subsets of
\mathcal{D}_{m}
:

\mathcal{B}_{m}:=\{d\in\mathcal{D}_{m}:\ U_{m}^{0}(d)>0\},\qquad\mathcal{W}_{m}:=\{d\in\mathcal{D}_{m}:\ U_{m}^{0}(d)<0\}.

These are nonempty because
d(L_{m}^{+})\in\mathcal{B}_{m}
and
d(L_{m}^{-})\in\mathcal{W}_{m}
. Now define subsets of
X_{m}^{0}
:

\mathcal{U}_{m}:=\mathcal{B}_{m}+C_{m}^{\circ},\qquad\mathcal{V}_{m}:=\mathcal{W}_{m}-C_{m}^{\circ}.

Because
C_{m}^{\circ}
is open in
X_{m}^{0}
and a cone,
\mathcal{U}_{m}
and
\mathcal{V}_{m}
are open, convex, and nonempty.

We claim that they are disjoint sets. Suppose for contradiction that there exists
z\in\mathcal{U}_{m}\cap\mathcal{V}_{m}
.
Then there exist
d_{b}\in\mathcal{B}_{m}
,
d_{w}\in\mathcal{W}_{m}
and
c,c^{\prime}\in C_{m}^{\circ}
such that

z=d_{b}+c=d_{w}-c^{\prime}.

Let
L_{b}:=L_{m}^{0}+d_{b}\in\mathcal{K}_{m}
and
L_{w}:=L_{m}^{0}+d_{w}\in\mathcal{K}_{m}
. Then

L_{w}-L_{b}=d_{w}-d_{b}=c+c^{\prime}\in C_{m},

so
L_{w}(u)\geq L_{b}(u)
for all
u\in[0,1]
. By weak dominance,
L_{w}\succsim L_{b}
, hence

U_{m}(L_{w})\geq U_{m}(L_{b})
because
U_{m}
represents
\succsim
on
\mathcal{K}_{m}
.
But
U_{m}(L_{w})=U_{m}^{0}(d_{w})<0
and
U_{m}(L_{b})=U_{m}^{0}(d_{b})>0
, contradiction. Thus

\mathcal{U}_{m}\cap\mathcal{V}_{m}=\varnothing.

(18)

Step 3: Hahn–Banach separation on
X_{m}^{0}
; positivity on the cone.

Apply Hahn–Banach Separation Lemma
6
in the normed space
(X_{m}^{0},\|\cdot\|_{\infty})

to the disjoint nonempty convex sets
\mathcal{U}_{m}
and
\mathcal{V}_{m}
.
Since
\mathcal{U}_{m}
is open,
\operatorname{int}\mathcal{U}_{m}=\mathcal{U}_{m}\neq\varnothing
. Hence there exist a nonzero
continuous linear functional
\widetilde{F}_{m}:X_{m}^{0}\to\mathbb{R}
and
\gamma_{m}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\widetilde{F}_{m}(u)\geq\gamma_{m}\geq\widetilde{F}_{m}(v)\qquad(\forall u\in\mathcal{U}_{m},\ \forall v\in\mathcal{V}_{m}).

(19)

(3.1)
\widetilde{F}_{m}\geq 0
on
C_{m}^{\circ}
.

Fix
c\in C_{m}^{\circ}
and any
d_{b}\in\mathcal{B}_{m}
.
For every
\lambda>0
we have
d_{b}+\lambda c\in\mathcal{U}_{m}
(since
C_{m}^{\circ}
is a cone),
so by (
19
),

\widetilde{F}_{m}(d_{b})+\lambda\widetilde{F}_{m}(c)=\widetilde{F}_{m}(d_{b}+\lambda c)\geq\gamma_{m}\qquad(\forall\lambda>0).

If
\widetilde{F}_{m}(c)<0
, the left side tends to
-\infty
as
\lambda\to\infty
, impossible. Thus

\widetilde{F}_{m}(c)\geq 0\qquad(\forall c\in C_{m}^{\circ}).

(20)

(3.2) Sign inequalities on
\mathcal{B}_{m}
and
\mathcal{W}_{m}
.

Fix some
c_{0}\in C_{m}^{\circ}
. Let
d_{b}\in\mathcal{B}_{m}
. For every
\lambda>0
,

d_{b}+\lambda c_{0}\in\mathcal{U}_{m}
, so (
19
) yields

\widetilde{F}_{m}(d_{b}+\lambda c_{0})=\widetilde{F}_{m}(d_{b})+\lambda\widetilde{F}_{m}(c_{0})\geq\gamma_{m}\qquad(\forall\lambda>0),

equivalently

\widetilde{F}_{m}(d_{b})\geq\gamma_{m}-\lambda\widetilde{F}_{m}(c_{0})\qquad(\forall\lambda>0).

By (
20
),
\widetilde{F}_{m}(c_{0})\geq 0
; letting
\lambda\to 0^{+}
gives

\widetilde{F}_{m}(d_{b})\geq\gamma_{m}.

Similarly, for
d_{w}\in\mathcal{W}_{m}
and every
\lambda>0
we have
d_{w}-\lambda c_{0}\in\mathcal{V}_{m}
, hence

\widetilde{F}_{m}(d_{w}-\lambda c_{0})=\widetilde{F}_{m}(d_{w})-\lambda\widetilde{F}_{m}(c_{0})\leq\gamma_{m}\qquad(\forall\lambda>0),

so letting
\lambda\to 0^{+}
again yields
\widetilde{F}_{m}(d_{w})\leq\gamma_{m}
. Therefore

\widetilde{F}_{m}(d)\geq\gamma_{m}\ (d\in\mathcal{B}_{m}),\qquad\widetilde{F}_{m}(d)\leq\gamma_{m}\ (d\in\mathcal{W}_{m}).

(21)

Step 4: Constancy of
\widetilde{F}_{m}
on
\{U_{m}^{0}=0\}
.

Let
d\in\mathcal{D}_{m}
with
U_{m}^{0}(d)=0
. Fix any
d^{+}\in\mathcal{B}_{m}
and
d^{-}\in\mathcal{W}_{m}
.
For each
\lambda\in(0,1)
,

U_{m}^{0}\bigl((1-\lambda)d+\lambda d^{+}\bigr)=(1-\lambda)\cdot 0+\lambda U_{m}^{0}(d^{+})>0,

so
(1-\lambda)d+\lambda d^{+}\in\mathcal{B}_{m}
; similarly
(1-\lambda)d+\lambda d^{-}\in\mathcal{W}_{m}
.
By (
21
),

\widetilde{F}_{m}\bigl((1-\lambda)d+\lambda d^{+}\bigr)\geq\gamma_{m},\qquad\widetilde{F}_{m}\bigl((1-\lambda)d+\lambda d^{-}\bigr)\leq\gamma_{m}.

Expanding by linearity and letting
\lambda\downarrow 0
gives

\widetilde{F}_{m}(d)\geq\gamma_{m},\qquad\widetilde{F}_{m}(d)\leq\gamma_{m},

hence

U_{m}^{0}(d)=0\ \Longrightarrow\ \widetilde{F}_{m}(d)=\gamma_{m}.

(22)

In particular
0\in\mathcal{D}_{m}
(take
L=L_{m}^{0}
), and
U_{m}^{0}(0)=0
, so (
22
) yields

0=\widetilde{F}_{m}(0)=\gamma_{m}.

Thus, rewriting (
21
) with
\gamma_{m}=0
,

\widetilde{F}_{m}(d)\geq 0\ (d\in\mathcal{B}_{m}),\qquad\widetilde{F}_{m}(d)\leq 0\ (d\in\mathcal{W}_{m}),\qquad\widetilde{F}_{m}(c)\geq 0\ (c\in C_{m}^{\circ}),

(23)

and
\widetilde{F}_{m}(d)=0
whenever
U_{m}^{0}(d)=0
.

Step 5: Proportionality
\widetilde{F}_{m}=a(m)\,U_{m}^{0}
on
\mathcal{D}_{m}
and Interior ball.

(5.1) Proportionality on
\mathcal{D}_{m}
.

Let
I_{m}:=U_{m}^{0}(\mathcal{D}_{m})\subset\mathbb{R}
. Since
\mathcal{D}_{m}
is convex and
U_{m}^{0}
is affine,
I_{m}
is an interval.
Define
\varphi_{m}:I_{m}\to\mathbb{R}
by

\varphi_{m}(y):=\widetilde{F}_{m}(d)\quad\text{for any }d\in\mathcal{D}_{m}\text{ with }U_{m}^{0}(d)=y.

We show
\varphi_{m}
is well-defined. Let
d_{1},d_{2}\in\mathcal{D}_{m}
satisfy
U_{m}^{0}(d_{1})=U_{m}^{0}(d_{2})=y
.

If
y=0
, then
\widetilde{F}_{m}(d_{1})=\widetilde{F}_{m}(d_{2})=0
by (
22
) (with
\gamma_{m}=0
).

Assume
y>0
. Choose any
d^{-}\in\mathcal{W}_{m}
and set

\lambda(y;d^{-}):=\frac{y}{y-U_{m}^{0}(d^{-})}\in(0,1),

so that

U_{m}^{0}\bigl((1-\lambda(y;d^{-}))d_{i}+\lambda(y;d^{-})d^{-}\bigr)=0\qquad(i=1,2).

By (
22
) and linearity,

0=\widetilde{F}_{m}\bigl((1-\lambda)d_{i}+\lambda d^{-}\bigr)=(1-\lambda)\widetilde{F}_{m}(d_{i})+\lambda\widetilde{F}_{m}(d^{-})\qquad(i=1,2),

hence
\widetilde{F}_{m}(d_{1})=\widetilde{F}_{m}(d_{2})
. The case
y<0
is identical using a fixed
d^{+}\in\mathcal{B}_{m}
.

Therefore
\varphi_{m}
is well-defined. It is affine on
I_{m}
because for
y_{i}\in I_{m}
and
d_{i}\in\mathcal{D}_{m}

with
U_{m}^{0}(d_{i})=y_{i}
, we have for
\theta\in[0,1]
:

U_{m}^{0}\bigl((1-\theta)d_{1}+\theta d_{2}\bigr)=(1-\theta)y_{1}+\theta y_{2},\qquad\widetilde{F}_{m}\bigl((1-\theta)d_{1}+\theta d_{2}\bigr)=(1-\theta)\widetilde{F}_{m}(d_{1})+\theta\widetilde{F}_{m}(d_{2}).

Also
\varphi_{m}(0)=0
. Hence there exists
a(m)\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\widetilde{F}_{m}(d)=a(m)\,U_{m}^{0}(d)\qquad(\forall d\in\mathcal{D}_{m}).

(24)

Moreover
a(m)\geq 0
because for any
d^{+}\in\mathcal{B}_{m}
,

0\leq\widetilde{F}_{m}(d^{+})=a(m)\,U_{m}^{0}(d^{+}),\qquad U_{m}^{0}(d^{+})>0.

(5.2) Interior point and radius in
\mathcal{D}_{m}
.

For
j=0,\dots,n-1
define the corner Lorenz curves

K_{m,j}(u):=\max\!\left\{0,\ \frac{u-t_{j}}{1-t_{j}}\right\}\in\mathcal{L}_{m}.

Pick any strictly positive weight vector

\lambda=(\lambda_{0},\dots,\lambda_{n-1})\in\Delta_{n-1}^{\circ}:=\Bigl\{\lambda_{j}>0,\ \sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\lambda_{j}=1\Bigr\},

and define

S_{m}(u;\lambda):=\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\lambda_{j}\,K_{m,j}(u)\in X_{m}.

Define discrete differences for
f\in X_{m}
:

\Delta_{k}f:=f(t_{k})-f(t_{k-1})\quad(k=1,\dots,n),\qquad\Delta^{2}_{k}f:=\Delta_{k+1}f-\Delta_{k}f=f(t_{k+1})-2f(t_{k})+f(t_{k-1})\quad(k=1,\dots,n-1).

Then for
L\in X_{m}
with
L(0)=0
,
L(1)=1
,

L\in\mathcal{L}_{m}\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \Delta_{k}L\geq 0\ (k=1,\dots,n)\ \text{and}\ \Delta^{2}_{k}L\geq 0\ (k=1,\dots,n-1),

since on
[t_{k-1},t_{k}]
the slope equals
n\,\Delta_{k}L
and convexity means these slopes are nondecreasing.

A direct calculation gives for
K_{m,j}
:

\Delta_{k}K_{m,j}=\begin{cases}0,&k\leq j,\\[3.0pt]
\dfrac{1}{n-j},&k\geq j+1,\end{cases}\qquad(k=1,\dots,n),

hence

\Delta_{k}S_{m}(\cdot;\lambda)=\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\frac{\lambda_{j}}{n-j},\qquad\Delta^{2}_{k}S_{m}(\cdot;\lambda)=\frac{\lambda_{k}}{n-k}.

Let

\lambda_{*}(\lambda):=\min_{0\leq j\leq n-1}\lambda_{j}>0,\qquad s(n;\lambda):=\frac{\lambda_{*}(\lambda)}{n}.

Then for all
k
,

\Delta_{k}S_{m}(\cdot;\lambda)\geq\Delta_{1}S_{m}(\cdot;\lambda)=\frac{\lambda_{0}}{n}\geq s(n;\lambda),\qquad\Delta^{2}_{k}S_{m}(\cdot;\lambda)=\frac{\lambda_{k}}{n-k}\geq s(n;\lambda).

Now let
h\in X_{m}^{0}
with
\|h\|_{\infty}<r
. Then for all
k
,

|\Delta_{k}h|\leq|h(t_{k})|+|h(t_{k-1})|\leq 2\|h\|_{\infty}<2r,\qquad|\Delta^{2}_{k}h|\leq|h(t_{k+1})|+2|h(t_{k})|+|h(t_{k-1})|\leq 4\|h\|_{\infty}<4r.

Choose the interior radius

r(n;\lambda):=\frac{s(n;\lambda)}{8}=\frac{\lambda_{*}(\lambda)}{8n}.

For any
\|h\|_{\infty}<r(n;\lambda)
we then have, for all
k
,

\Delta_{k}\bigl(S_{m}(\cdot;\lambda)+h\bigr)\geq s(n;\lambda)-2r(n;\lambda)=\frac{3}{4}\,s(n;\lambda)>0,

and for
k=1,\dots,n-1
,

\Delta^{2}_{k}\bigl(S_{m}(\cdot;\lambda)+h\bigr)\geq s(n;\lambda)-4r(n;\lambda)=\frac{1}{2}\,s(n;\lambda)>0.

Since also

\bigl(S_{m}(\cdot;\lambda)+h\bigr)(0)=0,\qquad\bigl(S_{m}(\cdot;\lambda)+h\bigr)(1)=1,

it follows that
S_{m}(\cdot;\lambda)+h
is strictly increasing and convex.
Hence

S_{m}(\cdot;\lambda)+h\in\mathcal{K}_{m}.

In particular, taking
h=0
gives
S_{m}(\cdot;\lambda)\in\mathcal{K}_{m}
. Therefore

B_{X_{m}^{0}}\bigl(S_{m}(\cdot;\lambda)-L_{m}^{0},\ r(n;\lambda)\bigr)\subset\mathcal{D}_{m},\qquad\operatorname{int}_{X_{m}^{0}}(\mathcal{D}_{m})\neq\varnothing.

(25)

(5.3)
a(m)>0
.

Assume for contradiction that
a(m)=0
. Then (
24
) implies
\widetilde{F}_{m}(d)=0
for all
d\in\mathcal{D}_{m}
,
hence
\widetilde{F}_{m}
vanishes on the open ball in (
25
). Let

d_{0}:=S_{m}(\cdot;\lambda)-L_{m}^{0}\in\mathcal{D}_{m},

so
\widetilde{F}_{m}(d_{0})=0
. Fix arbitrary
x\in X_{m}^{0}
. If
x\neq 0
, choose
q>0
such that
q\|x\|_{\infty}<r(n;\lambda)
.
Then
d_{0}+qx\in B(d_{0},r(n;\lambda))\subset\mathcal{D}_{m}
, so

0=\widetilde{F}_{m}(d_{0}+qx)=\widetilde{F}_{m}(d_{0})+q\widetilde{F}_{m}(x)=q\widetilde{F}_{m}(x),

hence
\widetilde{F}_{m}(x)=0
. Since
x\in X_{m}^{0}
was arbitrary,
\widetilde{F}_{m}\equiv 0
on
X_{m}^{0}
, contradicting separation
(which gave
\widetilde{F}_{m}\neq 0
). Hence
a(m)\neq 0
, and with
a(m)\geq 0
we obtain
a(m)>0
.

Define the normalized functional

F_{m}:=\frac{1}{a(m)}\,\widetilde{F}_{m}.

Then
F_{m}
is continuous linear on
X_{m}^{0}
and by (
24
) we have

F_{m}(d)=U_{m}^{0}(d)\qquad(\forall d\in\mathcal{D}_{m}).

(26)

Consequently, for
L\in\mathcal{K}_{m}
,

F_{m}(L-L_{m}^{0})=U_{m}^{0}(L-L_{m}^{0})=U_{m}(L)=U(L)-U(L_{m}^{0}),

and
L\mapsto F_{m}(L-L_{m}^{0})
represents
\succsim
on
\mathcal{K}_{m}
.

Step 6: Step–kernel representation on the fixed grid.

Every
h\in X_{m}^{0}
has the hat expansion (
15
). Define

\alpha(m,j):=F_{m}(H_{m,j})\qquad(j=1,\dots,n-1).

Then by linearity,

F_{m}(h)=\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\alpha(m,j)\,h(t_{j})\qquad(\forall h\in X_{m}^{0}).

(27)

(6.1) Abel summation and a right–continuous step kernel.

Define tail sums

p(m,k):=\sum_{j=k}^{n-1}\alpha(m,j)\quad(k=1,\dots,n-1),\qquad p(m,n):=0,

and define the right–continuous step function
p_{m}:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
by

p_{m}(u):=p(m,k)\quad\text{for }u\in[t_{k-1},t_{k})\ (k=1,\dots,n),\qquad p_{m}(1):=p(m,n)=0.

For
g\in X_{m}
define the grid Riemann–Stieltjes sum

\int_{0}^{1}p_{m}(u)\,dg(u):=\sum_{k=1}^{n}p(m,k)\bigl(g(t_{k})-g(t_{k-1})\bigr).

(28)

Let
h\in X_{m}^{0}
, and write
h_{k}:=h(t_{k})
for
k=0,\dots,n
. Then

h_{0}=h_{n}=0
. Since

p(m,k)-p(m,k+1)=\alpha(m,k)\qquad(k=1,\dots,n-1),

we compute

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}p_{m}\,dh

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}p(m,k)(h_{k}-h_{k-1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}p(m,k)h_{k}-\sum_{k=1}^{n}p(m,k)h_{k-1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}p(m,k)h_{k}-\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}p(m,k+1)h_{k}\qquad(\text{because }h_{0}=h_{n}=0)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\bigl(p(m,k)-p(m,k+1)\bigr)h_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\alpha(m,k)\,h(t_{k}).

Combining this with (
27
), we obtain

F_{m}(h)=\int_{0}^{1}p_{m}\,dh\qquad(\forall h\in X_{m}^{0}).

(29)

(6.2) Difference form on
\mathcal{K}_{m}
.

Let
G,H\in\mathcal{K}_{m}
. Then
G-H\in X_{m}^{0}
, so by
(
29
),

F_{m}(G-H)=\int_{0}^{1}p_{m}\,d(G-H).

Also,

G-H=(G-L_{m}^{0})-(H-L_{m}^{0}),

and
G-L_{m}^{0},\ H-L_{m}^{0}\in\mathcal{D}_{m}
. Therefore, by linearity of
F_{m}

and (
26
),

\displaystyle F_{m}(G-H)

\displaystyle=F_{m}(G-L_{m}^{0})-F_{m}(H-L_{m}^{0})

\displaystyle=U_{m}^{0}(G-L_{m}^{0})-U_{m}^{0}(H-L_{m}^{0})

\displaystyle=U(G)-U(H).

Hence

U(G)-U(H)=\int_{0}^{1}p_{m}\,d(G-H)\qquad(\forall G,H\in\mathcal{K}_{m}).

(30)

In particular, the map

L\longmapsto F_{m}(L-L_{m}^{0})

represents
\succsim
on
\mathcal{K}_{m}
, because for
A,B\in\mathcal{K}_{m}
,

A\succsim B\Longleftrightarrow U(A)\geq U(B)\Longleftrightarrow F_{m}(A-L_{m}^{0})\geq F_{m}(B-L_{m}^{0}).

(6.3) Weak dominance
\Rightarrow
monotonicity of
p_{m}
.

Fix
\lambda\in\Delta_{n-1}^{\circ}
, and let

S_{m}:=S_{m}(\cdot;\lambda)\in\mathcal{K}_{m}

and
r(n;\lambda)>0
be as in Step 5.2, so that

\|h\|_{\infty}<r(n;\lambda)\quad\Longrightarrow\quad S_{m}+h\in\mathcal{K}_{m}\qquad(h\in X_{m}^{0}).

Fix
k\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
, and set

\varepsilon:=\frac{r(n;\lambda)}{2}>0.

Since
\|H_{m,k}\|_{\infty}=1
, we have

\|\varepsilon H_{m,k}\|_{\infty}=\varepsilon<r(n;\lambda),

hence

S_{m}+\varepsilon H_{m,k}\in\mathcal{K}_{m}.

Moreover
H_{m,k}\geq 0
pointwise, so

S_{m}+\varepsilon H_{m,k}\geq S_{m}\qquad\text{pointwise on }[0,1].

By weak dominance,

S_{m}+\varepsilon H_{m,k}\succsim S_{m}.

Since
L\mapsto F_{m}(L-L_{m}^{0})
represents
\succsim
on
\mathcal{K}_{m}
,

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq F_{m}\bigl((S_{m}+\varepsilon H_{m,k})-L_{m}^{0}\bigr)-F_{m}(S_{m}-L_{m}^{0})

\displaystyle=\varepsilon F_{m}(H_{m,k})=\varepsilon\alpha(m,k).

Therefore

\alpha(m,k)\geq 0\qquad(k=1,\dots,n-1).

(31)

Since

p(m,k)-p(m,k+1)=\alpha(m,k)\qquad(k=1,\dots,n-1),\qquad p(m,n)=0,

it follows that

p(m,1)\geq p(m,2)\geq\cdots\geq p(m,n)=0.

Hence
p_{m}
is nonincreasing on
[0,1]
.

Step 7: Finite kernel
\nu_{m}
and discrete integration by parts.

Define discrete drops

\alpha(m,k):=p(m,k)-p(m,k+1)\qquad(k=1,\dots,n-1).

(Equivalently
\alpha(m,k)=F_{m}(H_{m,k})
and
\alpha(m,k)\geq 0
by (
31
).)
Define a finite nonnegative Borel measure
\nu_{m}
on
[0,1]
by

\nu_{m}(\{t_{k}\}):=\alpha(m,k)\quad(k=1,\dots,n-1),\qquad\nu_{m}(\{0\})=0,\qquad\nu_{m}(\{1\})=0.

(32)

Its total mass is

\nu_{m}([0,1])=\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\alpha(m,k)=p(m,1)-p(m,n)=p(m,1)\geq 0.

(33)

For
G,H\in\mathcal{K}_{m}
, set

g:=G-H\in X_{m}^{0}.

Then
g(t_{0})=g(t_{n})=0
, and discrete integration by parts gives

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}p_{m}\,d(G-H)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}p(m,k)\bigl(g(t_{k})-g(t_{k-1})\bigr)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}p(m,k)g(t_{k})-\sum_{k=1}^{n}p(m,k)g(t_{k-1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}p(m,k)g(t_{k})-\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}p(m,k+1)g(t_{k})\qquad(\text{since }g(t_{0})=g(t_{n})=0,\ p(m,n)=0)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}g(t_{k})\bigl(p(m,k)-p(m,k+1)\bigr)=\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\bigl(G(t_{k})-H(t_{k})\bigr)\alpha(m,k)

\displaystyle=\int_{[0,1]}\bigl(G(u)-H(u)\bigr)\,\nu_{m}(du).

(34)

Combining (
30
) with (
34
), we obtain

U(G)-U(H)=\int_{0}^{1}p_{m}\,d(G-H)=\int_{[0,1]}\bigl(G(u)-H(u)\bigr)\,\nu_{m}(du)\qquad(\forall G,H\in\mathcal{K}_{m}).

This completes the proof.

∎

Theorem 6

(Lorenz–Stieltjes representation under weak dominance and Archimedean)
.

Let
\succsim
be a complete preorder on
\mathcal{L}
satisfying:

(A1)

Mixture independence:
Axiom
5
.

(A2)

Closed graph continuity:

The graph

\{(L_{1},L_{2})\in\mathcal{L}\times\mathcal{L}:\ L_{1}\succsim L_{2}\}

is closed in
\mathcal{L}\times\mathcal{L}
, where
\mathcal{L}
carries the topology induced by

(C([0,1]),\|\cdot\|_{\infty})
..

(A3)

Weak dominance:
if
L_{1}(u)\geq L_{2}(u)
for all
u\in[0,1]
, then
L_{1}\succsim L_{2}
.

(A4)

Nontriviality:
Axiom
4
on
\mathcal{L}
.

(A5)

Archimedean on Lorenz Domain:
Axiom
7
on
\mathcal{L}
.

Then there exist a finite nonnegative Borel measure
\nu
on
[0,1]
and a nonincreasing
bounded–variation kernel
p:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
, right–continuous on
[0,1)
, such that

p(u)=\nu((u,1])\qquad(0\leq u<1),

and fixing the endpoint value by

p(1)=p(1^{-}),\qquad p(1^{-}):=\lim_{t\uparrow 1}p(t),

one has

V(L):=\int_{[0,1]}L(u)\,\nu(du)=\int_{0}^{1}p(u)\,dL(u)\qquad(L\in\mathcal{L}).

Moreover, if
\widetilde{p}:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
is another nonincreasing bounded–variation
kernel, right–continuous on
[0,1)
, also satisfying:

\widetilde{p}(1)=\widetilde{p}(1^{-}),\qquad\widetilde{p}(1^{-}):=\lim_{t\uparrow 1}\widetilde{p}(t),

and such that

L_{1}\succsim L_{2}\Longleftrightarrow\int_{0}^{1}\widetilde{p}\,dL_{1}\geq\int_{0}^{1}\widetilde{p}\,dL_{2}\qquad(\forall L_{1},L_{2}\in\mathcal{L}),

then there exist
a>0
and
b\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\widetilde{p}(u)=a\,p(u)+b\qquad(u\in[0,1]).

Proof.

By Proposition
1
(using (A1), (A2), (A4)), fix an affine

\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
–continuous representation
U:\mathcal{L}\to\mathbb{R}
of
\succsim
.
By (A4) (nontriviality),
U
is nonconstant.

Dyadic setup.

For each
m\in\mathbb{N}
let

n_{m}:=2^{m},\qquad t_{m,k}:=\frac{k}{n_{m}}\qquad(k=0,1,\dots,n_{m}),

and define

X_{m}:=\Bigl\{g\in C([0,1]):g\text{ is affine on each }[t_{m,k-1},t_{m,k}]\ (k=1,\dots,n_{m})\Bigr\},\qquad\mathcal{L}_{m}:=\mathcal{L}\cap X_{m}.

Let
P_{m}:C([0,1])\to X_{m}
be polygonal interpolation on
\{t_{m,k}\}
; then
P_{m}f\to f
uniformly for every

f\in C([0,1])
, and
P_{m}L\in\mathcal{L}_{m}
for every
L\in\mathcal{L}
.

Finite-grid representation.

Apply Theorem
5
with
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{L}
to
U

(weak dominance is (A3)).
Then there exists
m_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for every
m\geq m_{0}
there exist
a nonincreasing right–continuous dyadic step kernel
p_{m}

and a finite nonnegative Borel measure
\nu_{m}
supported on the interior grid

\{t_{m,1},\dots,t_{m,n_{m}-1}\}
such that for all
L,H\in\mathcal{L}_{m}
,

U(L)-U(H)=\int_{[0,1]}(L-H)\,d\nu_{m}=\int_{0}^{1}p_{m}\,d(L-H).

(35)

For the remainder of Step 1 fix
m\geq m_{0}
and abbreviate

n:=n_{m}=2^{m},\qquad t_{k}:=t_{m,k}=\frac{k}{n}\qquad(k=0,1,\dots,n).

Write the step kernel values as

p_{m}(u)=p(m,k)\quad\text{for }u\in[t_{k-1},t_{k})\qquad(k=1,\dots,n).

Since
\nu_{m}
is supported on
\{t_{1},\dots,t_{n-1}\}
, if
u\in[t_{n-1},1)
then

(u,1]\cap\operatorname{supp}\nu_{m}=\varnothing,

hence

p_{m}(u)=\nu_{m}((u,1])=0.

Therefore

p(m,n)=0.

If
u\in[0,t_{1})
, then

(u,1]\cap\operatorname{supp}\nu_{m}=\{t_{1},\dots,t_{n-1}\},

so

p(m,1)=p_{m}(u)=\nu_{m}((u,1])=\nu_{m}([0,1]).

Hence

\nu_{m}([0,1])=p(m,1).

(36)

Step 1: Uniform Mass Bound for
\nu_{m}
.

Assume (A5). Let
E(u):=u
(so
E\in\mathcal{L}_{m}
for every
m
). For
j=0,\dots,n-1
define the dyadic
corner Lorenz curves

K_{m,j}(u):=\max\!\left\{0,\frac{u-t_{j}}{1-t_{j}}\right\}\in\mathcal{L}_{m}.

Then
E\geq K_{m,1}\geq K_{m,n-1}
pointwise, hence by weak dominance (A3),

U(E)\geq U(K_{m,1})\geq U(K_{m,n-1}).

(37)

(1.1) Reference curve
K_{m,n-1}
.

For
K_{m,n-1}
we have
\Delta_{k}K_{m,n-1}=0
for
k=1,\dots,n-1
and
\Delta_{n}K_{m,n-1}=1
, hence

\int_{0}^{1}p_{m}\,dK_{m,n-1}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}p(m,k)\Delta_{k}K_{m,n-1}=p(m,n)\cdot 1=0.

Also, since
\nu_{m}
is supported on
\{t_{1},\dots,t_{n-1}\}
and

K_{m,n-1}(t_{k})=0\qquad(k=1,\dots,n-1),

we have

\int_{[0,1]}K_{m,n-1}(u)\,\nu_{m}(du)=0.

c_{m}:=U(K_{m,n-1}).

(38)

Applying (
35
) with
H=K_{m,n-1}
gives, for every
L\in\mathcal{L}_{m}
,

\displaystyle U(L)-c_{m}

\displaystyle=U(L)-U(K_{m,n-1})

\displaystyle=\int_{[0,1]}\bigl(L(u)-K_{m,n-1}(u)\bigr)\,\nu_{m}(du)=\int_{0}^{1}p_{m}\,d\bigl(L-K_{m,n-1}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\int_{[0,1]}L(u)\,\nu_{m}(du)=\int_{0}^{1}p_{m}\,dL(u).

(39)

(1.2) Express
p(m,1)=\nu_{m}([0,1])
in terms of
U(E)-U(K_{m,1})
and
U(E)-U(K_{m,n-1})
.

Using (
39
) with
L=E
and
\Delta_{k}E=1/n
for all
k
,

U(E)-c_{m}=\int_{0}^{1}p_{m}\,dE=\sum_{k=1}^{n}p(m,k)\frac{1}{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}p(m,k),

so

\sum_{k=1}^{n}p(m,k)=n\bigl(U(E)-c_{m}\bigr).

(40)

For
K_{m,1}
we have
K_{m,1}(t_{0})=K_{m,1}(t_{1})=0
and

K_{m,1}(t_{k})=(t_{k}-t_{1})/(1-t_{1})=(k-1)/(n-1)
for
k\geq 2
, hence

\Delta_{1}K_{m,1}=0,\qquad\Delta_{k}K_{m,1}=\frac{1}{n-1}\quad(k=2,\dots,n),

and therefore

U(K_{m,1})-c_{m}=\int_{0}^{1}p_{m}\,dK_{m,1}=\sum_{k=2}^{n}p(m,k)\frac{1}{n-1}.

Equivalently,

\sum_{k=2}^{n}p(m,k)=(n-1)\bigl(U(K_{m,1})-c_{m}\bigr).

(41)

Subtracting (
41
) from (
40
) yields

\displaystyle p(m,1)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}p(m,k)-\sum_{k=2}^{n}p(m,k)

\displaystyle=\bigl(U(E)-c_{m}\bigr)+(n-1)\bigl(U(E)-U(K_{m,1})\bigr).

(42)

Using (
38
) to eliminate
c_{m}
, we may rewrite (
42
) as

p(m,1)=\bigl(U(E)-U(K_{m,n-1})\bigr)+(n-1)\bigl(U(E)-U(K_{m,1})\bigr).

(43)

By (
36
), this is exactly the total mass
\nu_{m}([0,1])
.

(1.3) Global comparison bound from Axiom
7
.

Let
B\in\mathcal{L}
and
C,\delta>0
be given by Axiom
7
, and set

\Delta_{B}:=U(E)-U(B)>0.

We claim that

U(E)-U(L)\leq C\Delta_{B}\,\|E-L\|_{\infty}\qquad(\forall L\in\mathcal{L}).

(44)

Indeed, if
0<\|E-L\|_{\infty}<\delta
, then by Axiom
7
there exists

t_{L}\in[0,1]
such that

t_{L}\leq C\|E-L\|_{\infty}\qquad\text{and}\qquad L\succsim(1-t_{L})E+t_{L}B.

Applying the affine representation
U
gives

U(L)\geq(1-t_{L})U(E)+t_{L}U(B)=U(E)-t_{L}\Delta_{B},

hence

U(E)-U(L)\leq t_{L}\Delta_{B}\leq C\Delta_{B}\,\|E-L\|_{\infty}.

Now let
L\in\mathcal{L}
be arbitrary. If
L=E
, (
44
) is trivial. Otherwise define

\varepsilon:=\min\Bigl\{1,\frac{\delta}{2\|E-L\|_{\infty}}\Bigr\}\in(0,1],\qquad L^{\varepsilon}:=(1-\varepsilon)E+\varepsilon L.

Then

\|E-L^{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}=\varepsilon\|E-L\|_{\infty}<\delta,

so the previous estimate applied to
L^{\varepsilon}
yields

U(E)-U(L^{\varepsilon})\leq C\Delta_{B}\,\|E-L^{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}=C\Delta_{B}\,\varepsilon\|E-L\|_{\infty}.

Since

U(L^{\varepsilon})=(1-\varepsilon)U(E)+\varepsilon U(L),

we have

U(E)-U(L^{\varepsilon})=\varepsilon\bigl(U(E)-U(L)\bigr).

Cancelling
\varepsilon>0
proves (
44
).

(1.4) Apply (
44
) to
K_{m,1}
and
K_{m,n-1}
and conclude.

Since

\|E-K_{m,1}\|_{\infty}=\frac{1}{n},\qquad\|E-K_{m,n-1}\|_{\infty}=\frac{n-1}{n}\leq 1,

equation (
44
) gives

U(E)-U(K_{m,1})\leq\frac{C\Delta_{B}}{n},\qquad U(E)-U(K_{m,n-1})\leq C\Delta_{B}.

Substituting these bounds into (
43
), we obtain

\nu_{m}([0,1])=p(m,1)\leq C\Delta_{B}+(n-1)\frac{C\Delta_{B}}{n}\leq 2C\Delta_{B}.

Thus

0\leq\nu_{m}([0,1])\leq 2C\Delta_{B}\qquad(\forall m\geq m_{0}).

(45)

Also, since
c_{m}=U(K_{m,n-1})
, the same estimate yields

U(E)-C\Delta_{B}\leq c_{m}\leq U(E)\qquad(\forall m\geq m_{0}).

(46)

Step 2: Countable dense dyadic–rational class in
C([0,1])
.

From this point onward,
m
becomes a free variable again. For each
m\in\mathbb{N}
set

n_{m}:=2^{m},\qquad t_{m,k}:=\frac{k}{n_{m}}\qquad(k=0,1,\dots,n_{m}),

and let

X_{m}:=\Bigl\{g\in C([0,1]):g\text{ is affine on each }[t_{m,k-1},t_{m,k}]\ (k=1,\dots,n_{m})\Bigr\}.

For
g\in X_{m}
define the nodal vector

\mathbf{g}^{(m)}:=(g(t_{m,0}),g(t_{m,1}),\dots,g(t_{m,n_{m}}))\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{m}+1}.

Then
g\mapsto\mathbf{g}^{(m)}
is a bijection between
X_{m}
and
\mathbb{R}^{n_{m}+1}
, since a piecewise affine function
is uniquely determined by its nodal values. Moreover, for each
g\in X_{m}
,

\|g\|_{\infty}=\max_{0\leq k\leq n_{m}}|g(t_{m,k})|.

(47)

Indeed, on each interval
[t_{m,k-1},t_{m,k}]
the function
g
is affine, hence its maximum and minimum are attained at endpoints;
taking the maximum over all subintervals yields (
47
).

Define the dyadic–rational subclass

\mathscr{D}_{m}:=\Bigl\{g\in X_{m}:\ g(t_{m,k})\in\mathbb{Q}\ \text{for all }k=0,1,\dots,n_{m}\Bigr\},\qquad\mathscr{D}:=\bigcup_{m=1}^{\infty}\mathscr{D}_{m}\subset C([0,1]).

Claim 2.1:
\mathscr{D}
is countable.

Fix
m
. The nodal map
g\mapsto\mathbf{g}^{(m)}
identifies
\mathscr{D}_{m}
with
\mathbb{Q}^{n_{m}+1}
.
Since
\mathbb{Q}
is countable and
n_{m}+1<\infty
, the product
\mathbb{Q}^{n_{m}+1}
is countable, hence
\mathscr{D}_{m}
is countable.
Therefore
\mathscr{D}=\bigcup_{m\geq 1}\mathscr{D}_{m}
is a countable union of countable sets which is countable according to basic set theory.

Claim 2.2:
\mathscr{D}
is dense in
C([0,1])
under
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
.

Let
f\in C([0,1])
and
\varepsilon>0
be given. For each
m
define the polygonal interpolation operator

P_{m}:C([0,1])\to X_{m}
by

(P_{m}f)(t_{m,k})=f(t_{m,k})\qquad(k=0,1,\dots,n_{m}),\quad\text{and}\quad P_{m}f\text{ affine on each }[t_{m,k-1},t_{m,k}].

Since
f
is uniformly continuous,
\|P_{m}f-f\|_{\infty}\to 0
as
m\to\infty
.
Choose
m
so large that

\|P_{m}f-f\|_{\infty}<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}.

For each node
t_{m,k}
choose a rational
q_{k}\in\mathbb{Q}
such that

|q_{k}-(P_{m}f)(t_{m,k})|<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\qquad(k=0,1,\dots,n_{m}),

which is possible since
\mathbb{Q}
is dense in
\mathbb{R}
.
Let
g\in X_{m}
be the unique polygonal function satisfying
g(t_{m,k})=q_{k}
for all
k
.
Then
g\in\mathscr{D}_{m}\subset\mathscr{D}
. Moreover,
g-P_{m}f\in X_{m}
, hence by (
47
),

\|g-P_{m}f\|_{\infty}=\max_{0\leq k\leq n_{m}}\bigl|g(t_{m,k})-(P_{m}f)(t_{m,k})\bigr|=\max_{0\leq k\leq n_{m}}|q_{k}-(P_{m}f)(t_{m,k})|<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}.

Therefore

\|g-f\|_{\infty}\leq\|g-P_{m}f\|_{\infty}+\|P_{m}f-f\|_{\infty}<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}=\varepsilon,

which proves density.

Since
\mathscr{D}
is countable, fix an enumeration

\mathscr{D}=\{d_{1},d_{2},d_{3},\dots\}.

(48)

Step 3: Uniformly Bounded Positive Functionals and a diagonal subsequence.

For each
m\geq m_{0}
let
\nu_{m}
be as above and let
c_{m}
be the corresponding constant from Step 1, so that

U(L)-c_{m}=\int_{[0,1]}L\,d\nu_{m}\qquad(\forall L\in\mathcal{L}_{m}),

and the total masses are uniformly bounded:

0\leq\nu_{m}([0,1])\leq M_{\nu}\qquad(\forall m\geq m_{0}),

with
M_{\nu}<\infty
fixed.

Define the positive continuous linear functionals
T_{m}:C([0,1])\to\mathbb{R}
by

T_{m}(f):=\int_{[0,1]}f\,d\nu_{m}\qquad(f\in C([0,1])).

(49)

Then for every
f\in C([0,1])
,

|T_{m}(f)|\leq\|f\|_{\infty}\,\nu_{m}([0,1]),

and since each
T_{m}
is positive,

\|T_{m}\|=\sup_{\|f\|_{\infty}\leq 1}|T_{m}(f)|=T_{m}(\mathbf{1})=\nu_{m}([0,1]).

Hence we have the uniform operator norm bound

\|T_{m}\|\leq M_{\nu}\qquad(\forall m\geq m_{0}).

(50)

Let
\mathscr{D}=\{d_{1},d_{2},\dots\}
be the fixed countable dense dyadic–rational class from Step 2,
with enumeration (
48
).
We now build a diagonal subsequence along which
T_{m}(d_{r})
converges for every
r
.

Initialize
m^{(0)}_{j}:=m_{0}+j-1
for
j\geq 1
. Inductively, given an increasing index sequence

(m^{(r-1)}_{j})_{j\geq 1}
, the scalar sequence
\bigl(T_{m^{(r-1)}_{j}}(d_{r})\bigr)_{j\geq 1}
is bounded by (
50
),
so it admits a convergent subsequence; choose an increasing subsequence
(m^{(r)}_{j})_{j\geq 1}
of
(m^{(r-1)}_{j})_{j\geq 1}

such that
\bigl(T_{m^{(r)}_{j}}(d_{r})\bigr)
converges.

Define the diagonal indices

m_{j}:=m^{(j)}_{j}\qquad(j\geq 1).

(51)

Then
(m_{j})
is strictly increasing, and for each fixed
r\in\mathbb{N}
, the tail
(m_{j})_{j\geq r}
is a subsequence of

(m^{(r)}_{j})
, hence

T_{m_{j}}(d_{r})\ \text{converges as }j\to\infty\qquad(\forall r\in\mathbb{N}).

(52)

Finally, Step 1 shows that
(c_{m})_{m\geq m_{0}}
is bounded. Passing to a further subsequence of
(m_{j})
(not relabeled),
we may assume that

c_{m_{j}}\to c\in\mathbb{R}.

(53)

The convergence property (
52
) is preserved under passing to subsequences.

Step 4: Limit functional
T
on
C([0,1])
.

We claim that for every
f\in C([0,1])
the real sequence
\bigl(T_{m_{j}}(f)\bigr)_{j\geq 1}
is Cauchy, hence convergent.

If
M_{\nu}=0
, then
\|T_{m_{j}}\|=0
for all
j
, so
T_{m_{j}}\equiv 0
and convergence is trivial. Hence assume
M_{\nu}>0
.
Fix
f\in C([0,1])
and
\varepsilon>0
. By density of
\mathscr{D}
, choose
r\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\|f-d_{r}\|_{\infty}<\frac{\varepsilon}{3M_{\nu}}.

Since
\bigl(T_{m_{j}}(d_{r})\bigr)
converges by (
52
), there exists
J
such that for all
j,j^{\prime}\geq J
,

|T_{m_{j}}(d_{r})-T_{m_{j^{\prime}}}(d_{r})|<\frac{\varepsilon}{3}.

Then for all
j,j^{\prime}\geq J
, using linearity and the operator norm bound (
50
),

\displaystyle|T_{m_{j}}(f)-T_{m_{j^{\prime}}}(f)|

\displaystyle\leq|T_{m_{j}}(f-d_{r})|+|T_{m_{j}}(d_{r})-T_{m_{j^{\prime}}}(d_{r})|+|T_{m_{j^{\prime}}}(d_{r}-f)|

\displaystyle\leq\|T_{m_{j}}\|\,\|f-d_{r}\|_{\infty}+\frac{\varepsilon}{3}+\|T_{m_{j^{\prime}}}\|\,\|f-d_{r}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq M_{\nu}\cdot\frac{\varepsilon}{3M_{\nu}}+\frac{\varepsilon}{3}+M_{\nu}\cdot\frac{\varepsilon}{3M_{\nu}}=\varepsilon.

Thus
\bigl(T_{m_{j}}(f)\bigr)
is Cauchy and hence converges in
\mathbb{R}
. Define

T(f):=\lim_{j\to\infty}T_{m_{j}}(f)\qquad(f\in C([0,1])).

(54)

In particular, for each
r
, the limit
T(d_{r})
agrees with the diagonal limit in (
52
); i.e. writing

\ell_{r}:=\lim_{j\to\infty}T_{m_{j}}(d_{r})\quad\text{we have}\quad T(d_{r})=\ell_{r}\qquad(r\in\mathbb{N}).

(55)

Linearity, boundedness, positivity.

For
f,g\in C([0,1])
and
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
,

T(\alpha f+\beta g)=\lim_{j\to\infty}T_{m_{j}}(\alpha f+\beta g)=\alpha\lim_{j\to\infty}T_{m_{j}}(f)+\beta\lim_{j\to\infty}T_{m_{j}}(g)=\alpha T(f)+\beta T(g),

so
T
is linear. Moreover, by (
50
),

|T(f)|=\lim_{j\to\infty}|T_{m_{j}}(f)|\leq\limsup_{j\to\infty}\|T_{m_{j}}\|\,\|f\|_{\infty}\leq M_{\nu}\,\|f\|_{\infty},

so
T
is bounded and hence continuous on
C([0,1])
. Finally, if
f\geq 0
then
T_{m_{j}}(f)\geq 0
for all
j

(since each
\nu_{m_{j}}
is nonnegative), hence
T(f)\geq 0
.

Step 5: Riesz–Stieltjes integral representation for
T
).

Apply Lemma
7
to the positive continuous linear functional
T:C([0,1])\to\mathbb{R}
.
Then there exists an increasing function
\alpha:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
(taken as right–continuous on
(0,1)

by the right–continuous modification) such that

T(f)=\int_{0}^{1}f\,d\alpha\qquad(\forall f\in C([0,1])).

(56)

Let
\nu
be the finite nonnegative Borel measure induced by
\alpha
, so that

\nu((a,b])=\alpha(b)-\alpha(a)\qquad(0\leq a<b\leq 1),

and hence

T(f)=\int_{[0,1]}f\,d\nu\qquad(\forall f\in C([0,1])).

(57)

Step 6: Identify
U
with the limit integral on
\mathcal{L}
.

Fix
L\in\mathcal{L}
and set
L^{(j)}:=P_{m_{j}}L\in\mathcal{L}_{m_{j}}
.
Then
\|L^{(j)}-L\|_{\infty}\to 0
as
j\to\infty
.
For each
j
, applying (
39
) to
m=m_{j}
and
L^{(j)}\in\mathcal{L}_{m_{j}}
yields

U\bigl(L^{(j)}\bigr)-c_{m_{j}}=\int_{[0,1]}L^{(j)}\,d\nu_{m_{j}}=T_{m_{j}}\!\bigl(L^{(j)}\bigr).

(58)

Since
U
is continuous on
(\mathcal{L},\|\cdot\|_{\infty})
by Proposition
1
(iii),

U\bigl(L^{(j)}\bigr)\to U(L).

Also
c_{m_{j}}\to c
by (
53
). It remains to show

T_{m_{j}}(L^{(j)})\to T(L)
.

Write

T_{m_{j}}\!\bigl(L^{(j)}\bigr)-T(L)=\Bigl(T_{m_{j}}\!\bigl(L^{(j)}\bigr)-T_{m_{j}}(L)\Bigr)+\Bigl(T_{m_{j}}(L)-T(L)\Bigr)=T_{m_{j}}\!\bigl(L^{(j)}-L\bigr)+\bigl(T_{m_{j}}(L)-T(L)\bigr).

The second term tends to
0
by (
54
). For the first term, (
50
) gives

\bigl|T_{m_{j}}(L^{(j)}-L)\bigr|\leq\|T_{m_{j}}\|\,\|L^{(j)}-L\|_{\infty}\leq M_{\nu}\,\|L^{(j)}-L\|_{\infty}\to 0.

Hence
T_{m_{j}}(L^{(j)})\to T(L)
. Passing to the limit in (
58
) yields

U(L)-c=T(L)=\int_{[0,1]}L\,d\nu\qquad(\forall L\in\mathcal{L}),

(59)

where the last equality uses (
57
).
Define

V(L):=\int_{[0,1]}L\,d\nu\qquad(L\in\mathcal{L}).

Then
V=U-c
on
\mathcal{L}
, and therefore
V
represents
\succsim
on
\mathcal{L}
.

Step 7: Tail kernel and Stieltjes form.

Define
p:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
by

p(u):=\nu((u,1])\qquad(0\leq u<1),\qquad p(1):=p(1^{-}),

where the left limit exists because the map
u\mapsto\nu((u,1])
is nonincreasing on
[0,1)
.

By continuity from above of the finite measure
\nu
,

p(1)=\lim_{t\uparrow 1}\nu((t,1])=\nu\!\left(\bigcap_{t<1}(t,1]\right)=\nu(\{1\}).

For
0\leq u<1
,

p(u)=\nu((u,1])=\nu((u,1))+\nu(\{1\})\geq\nu(\{1\})=p(1).

Hence
p
is nonincreasing on
[0,1]
.

Let
u\in[0,1)
and let
u_{n}\downarrow u
. Then
(u_{n},1]\downarrow(u,1]
, so continuity from above gives

p(u_{n})=\nu((u_{n},1])\longrightarrow\nu((u,1])=p(u).

Thus
p
is right–continuous on
[0,1)
. Since
p
is monotone, it belongs to
BV([0,1])
.

Let

\alpha_{\nu}(u):=\nu([0,u])\qquad(u\in[0,1]),

and define the auxiliary function

\widehat{p}(u):=\alpha_{\nu}(1)-\alpha_{\nu}(u)\qquad(u\in[0,1]).

Then for every
u\in[0,1)
,

\widehat{p}(u)=\nu([0,1])-\nu([0,u])=\nu((u,1])=p(u),

while

\widehat{p}(1)=\alpha_{\nu}(1)-\alpha_{\nu}(1)=0.

Hence
p-\widehat{p}
vanishes on
[0,1)
.

We claim that

\int_{0}^{1}p\,dL=\int_{0}^{1}\widehat{p}\,dL\qquad(\forall L\in\mathcal{L}).

Indeed, let
s:=p-\widehat{p}
. Then
s(u)=0
for
u\in[0,1)
, and

s(1)=p(1)-\widehat{p}(1)=\nu(\{1\}).

Since
s
differs from the zero function at only one point,
s\in BV([0,1])
. Fix
L\in\mathcal{L}
and
\varepsilon>0
. By continuity of
L
at
1
, choose
\delta\in(0,1)
such that

0\leq L(1)-L(1-\delta)<\varepsilon.

Let
P:0=t_{0}<\cdots<t_{m}=1
be any partition containing
1-\delta
, and let
\xi_{k}\in[t_{k-1},t_{k}]
.
Then the Riemann–Stieltjes sum

S(P,\xi;s,L):=\sum_{k=1}^{m}s(\xi_{k})\bigl(L(t_{k})-L(t_{k-1})\bigr)

has at most one nonzero term, and therefore

|S(P,\xi;s,L)|\leq|s(1)|\bigl(L(1)-L(1-\delta)\bigr)<|s(1)|\,\varepsilon.

Letting the mesh tend to
0
yields

\int_{0}^{1}s\,dL=0,

and hence

\int_{0}^{1}p\,dL=\int_{0}^{1}\widehat{p}\,dL.

Now fix
L\in\mathcal{L}
. Since
L
is continuous and of bounded variation, and
\alpha_{\nu}
is increasing,
the Riemann–Stieltjes integration–by–parts formula gives

\int_{0}^{1}L\,d\alpha_{\nu}+\int_{0}^{1}\alpha_{\nu}\,dL=L(1)\alpha_{\nu}(1)-L(0)\alpha_{\nu}(0).

Using
L(0)=0
and
L(1)=1
, we obtain

\int_{[0,1]}L(u)\,\nu(du)=\int_{0}^{1}L\,d\alpha_{\nu}=\alpha_{\nu}(1)-\int_{0}^{1}\alpha_{\nu}\,dL.

Also,

\int_{0}^{1}dL=L(1)-L(0)=1,

so

\int_{0}^{1}\widehat{p}(u)\,dL(u)=\int_{0}^{1}\bigl(\alpha_{\nu}(1)-\alpha_{\nu}(u)\bigr)\,dL(u)=\alpha_{\nu}(1)\int_{0}^{1}dL-\int_{0}^{1}\alpha_{\nu}\,dL=\alpha_{\nu}(1)-\int_{0}^{1}\alpha_{\nu}\,dL.

Therefore

\int_{[0,1]}L(u)\,\nu(du)=\int_{0}^{1}\widehat{p}(u)\,dL(u)=\int_{0}^{1}p(u)\,dL(u)\qquad(\forall L\in\mathcal{L}).

Combining this with (
59
), we obtain

U(L)-c=\int_{[0,1]}L(u)\,\nu(du)=\int_{0}^{1}p(u)\,dL(u)\qquad(\forall L\in\mathcal{L}).

Thus the functional

V(L):=\int_{[0,1]}L(u)\,\nu(du)=\int_{0}^{1}p(u)\,dL(u)\qquad(L\in\mathcal{L})

represents
\succsim
on
\mathcal{L}
.

Step 8: Uniqueness up to Positive Affine Transformation.

Let
p_{1}:=p
, and let
p_{2}:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
be another nonincreasing bounded–variation kernel,
right–continuous on
[0,1)
, satisfying

p_{2}(1)=p_{2}(1^{-}),

and such that

L_{1}\succsim L_{2}\Longleftrightarrow\int_{0}^{1}p_{2}\,dL_{1}\geq\int_{0}^{1}p_{2}\,dL_{2}\qquad(\forall L_{1},L_{2}\in\mathcal{L}).

For
i=1,2
, define

V_{i}(L):=\int_{0}^{1}p_{i}(u)\,dL(u)\qquad(L\in\mathcal{L}).

For
L_{1},L_{2}\in\mathcal{L}
and
\lambda\in[0,1]
, linearity of the Stieltjes integral gives

V_{i}\bigl((1-\lambda)L_{1}+\lambda L_{2}\bigr)=(1-\lambda)V_{i}(L_{1})+\lambda V_{i}(L_{2}).

Thus
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
are affine and represent the same nontrivial preorder on
\mathcal{L}
. Hence, by
Proposition
1
(iv), there exist constants
a>0
and
b\in\mathbb{R}
such that

V_{2}(L)=a\,V_{1}(L)+b\qquad(\forall L\in\mathcal{L}).

Equivalently,

\int_{0}^{1}p_{2}\,dL-a\int_{0}^{1}p_{1}\,dL-b\int_{0}^{1}dL=0\qquad(\forall L\in\mathcal{L}).

Since
\int_{0}^{1}dL=L(1)-L(0)=1
, this becomes

\int_{0}^{1}\bigl(p_{2}-ap_{1}-b\bigr)\,dL=0\qquad(\forall L\in\mathcal{L}).

(60)

Define

r:=p_{2}-ap_{1}-b.

Then
r\in BV([0,1])
,
r
is right–continuous on
[0,1)
, and

r(1)=r(1^{-}),

because both
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
satisfy the endpoint condition.

Fix
u\in[0,1)
and define

K_{u}(t):=\max\Bigl\{0,\frac{t-u}{1-u}\Bigr\}\qquad(t\in[0,1]).

Then
K_{u}\in\mathcal{L}
,
K_{u}
is constant on
[0,u]
, and affine on
[u,1]
with slope
1/(1-u)
.
Let
P:0=t_{0}<\cdots<t_{m}=1
be a partition such that
u=t_{j}
for some
j
, and let
\xi_{i}\in[t_{i-1},t_{i}]
.
The Riemann–Stieltjes sum for
\int_{0}^{1}r\,dK_{u}
is

S(P,\xi;r,K_{u}):=\sum_{i=1}^{m}r(\xi_{i})\bigl(K_{u}(t_{i})-K_{u}(t_{i-1})\bigr).

Since
K_{u}(t_{i})-K_{u}(t_{i-1})=0
for
i\leq j
, while

K_{u}(t_{i})-K_{u}(t_{i-1})=\frac{1}{1-u}(t_{i}-t_{i-1})\qquad(i\geq j+1),

we have

S(P,\xi;r,K_{u})=\frac{1}{1-u}\sum_{i=j+1}^{m}r(\xi_{i})\,(t_{i}-t_{i-1}).

Because
r\in BV([0,1])
, it has at most countably many discontinuities and is Riemann integrable on
[u,1]
.
Therefore, along partitions with mesh tending to
0
and containing
u
as a node,

\int_{0}^{1}r\,dK_{u}=\frac{1}{1-u}\int_{u}^{1}r(t)\,dt.

Applying (
60
) with
L=K_{u}
gives

\int_{u}^{1}r(t)\,dt=0\qquad(\forall u\in[0,1)).

(61)

Now fix
x\in[0,1)
and choose any
h\in(0,1-x)
. Then

\int_{x}^{x+h}r(t)\,dt=\int_{x}^{1}r(t)\,dt-\int_{x+h}^{1}r(t)\,dt=0.

Therefore

\displaystyle|r(x)|

\displaystyle=\left|r(x)-\frac{1}{h}\int_{x}^{x+h}r(t)\,dt\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{1}{h}\int_{x}^{x+h}\bigl(r(x)-r(t)\bigr)\,dt\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{t\in[x,x+h]}|r(x)-r(t)|.

Since
r
is right–continuous at
x
, the right–hand side tends to
0
as
h\downarrow 0
. Hence

r(x)=0\qquad(\forall x\in[0,1)).

Since
r(1)=r(1^{-})
, we also obtain

r(1)=\lim_{t\uparrow 1}r(t)=0.

Therefore

p_{2}(u)=a\,p_{1}(u)+b\qquad(\forall u\in[0,1]).

Since
p_{1}=p
and
p_{2}=\widetilde{p}
, this concludes our proof of Primal Theorem.

∎

Theorem 7

(Dual Lorenz–Stieltjes representation via dyadic discretization)
.

Let
\succsim
be a complete preorder on
\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}
satisfying:

(D1)

Dual independence:
Axiom
6
.

(D2)

Closed graph continuity:

The graph

\{(L_{1},L_{2})\in\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}\times\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}:\ L_{1}\succsim L_{2}\}

is closed in
\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}\times\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}
, where
\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}
carries the topology induced by

(C([0,1]),\|\cdot\|_{\infty})
.

(D3)

Weak dominance:

L_{1}\geq L_{2}
pointwise
\Rightarrow L_{1}\succsim L_{2}
.

(D4)

Nontriviality:
there exist
L^{+},L^{-}\in\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}
with
L^{+}\succ L^{-}
.

Define the involution operator
\mathcal{S}:\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}\to\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}
by

(\mathcal{S}L)(u):=1-L^{-1}(1-u),\qquad u\in[0,1],

and the transported preorder
\succsim^{\mathcal{S}}
on
\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}
by

M_{1}\succsim^{\mathcal{S}}M_{2}\iff\mathcal{S}M_{1}\succsim\mathcal{S}M_{2}.

Assume, in addition:

(D5)

Local Archimedean at equality after conjugacy:

the transported preorder
\succsim^{\mathcal{S}}
satisfies
Axiom
7
on
\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}
for some
anchor
B^{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}
such that

E\succ^{\mathcal{S}}B^{\mathcal{S}}.

Then there exist a finite nonnegative Borel measure
\nu
on
[0,1]
and a constant
c\in\mathbb{R}
such that, defining

q(u)=\nu([0,u])\qquad(0\leq u<1),

and fixing the endpoint value by

q(1)=q(1^{-}),\qquad q(1^{-}):=\lim_{t\uparrow 1}q(t),

the function
q
is nondecreasing, of bounded variation, right–continuous on
[0,1)
, and the functional

V(L):=c+\int_{0}^{1}q(u)\,d\bigl(L^{-1}(u)\bigr)\qquad(L\in\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow})

represents
\succsim
:

L_{1}\succsim L_{2}\ \Longleftrightarrow\ V(L_{1})\geq V(L_{2}).

Equivalently, there exists

c^{\prime}:=c+\nu([0,1])

such that

V(L)=c^{\prime}-\int_{[0,1]}L^{-1}(u)\,\nu(du)\qquad(L\in\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}).

Moreover, if
\widetilde{c}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\widetilde{q}:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
is another nondecreasing
bounded–variation kernel, right–continuous on
[0,1)
, satisfying

\widetilde{q}(1)=\widetilde{q}(1^{-}),\qquad\widetilde{q}(1^{-}):=\lim_{t\uparrow 1}\widetilde{q}(t),

and such that

\widetilde{V}(L):=\widetilde{c}+\int_{0}^{1}\widetilde{q}(u)\,d\bigl(L^{-1}(u)\bigr)\qquad(L\in\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow})

also represents
\succsim
on
\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}
, then there exist
a>0
and
b\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\widetilde{q}(u)=a\,q(u)+b\qquad(u\in[0,1]).

Proof.

Step 1: The Involution Operator
\mathcal{S}
.

For
L\in\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}
define

(\mathcal{S}L)(u):=1-L^{-1}(1-u)\qquad(u\in[0,1]).

(62)

(1.1)
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow})\subset\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}
.

Fix
L\in\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}
. Then
L^{-1}\in C([0,1])
is strictly increasing with
L^{-1}(0)=0
,
L^{-1}(1)=1
.
For
a,b\in[0,1]
and
\theta\in[0,1]
, set
x:=L^{-1}(a)
and
y:=L^{-1}(b)
. Convexity of
L
gives

L\bigl(\theta x+(1-\theta)y\bigr)\leq\theta L(x)+(1-\theta)L(y)=\theta a+(1-\theta)b.

Since
L^{-1}
is increasing,

\theta L^{-1}(a)+(1-\theta)L^{-1}(b)=\theta x+(1-\theta)y\leq L^{-1}\bigl(\theta a+(1-\theta)b\bigr),

so
L^{-1}
is concave. As
u\mapsto 1-u
is affine,
u\mapsto L^{-1}(1-u)
is concave, hence
u\mapsto-L^{-1}(1-u)
is convex,
and therefore
\mathcal{S}L
is convex. Also
L^{-1}(1-u)
is strictly decreasing, hence
\mathcal{S}L
is strictly increasing, and

(\mathcal{S}L)(0)=1-L^{-1}(1)=0,\qquad(\mathcal{S}L)(1)=1-L^{-1}(0)=1.

Thus
\mathcal{S}L\in\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}
.

(1.2) Involution.

Let
M:=\mathcal{S}L
. For
v\in[0,1]
,
v=M(u)
iff
1-v=L^{-1}(1-u)
iff
L(1-v)=1-u
iff

M^{-1}(v)=1-L(1-v).

(63)

Hence, for
u\in[0,1]
,

(\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{S}L))(u)=1-(\mathcal{S}L)^{-1}(1-u)=1-\bigl(1-L(u)\bigr)=L(u),

so
\mathcal{S}^{2}=\mathrm{id}
on
\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}
.

(1.3) Order Isomorphism.

If
L_{1},L_{2}\in\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}
satisfy
L_{1}\geq L_{2}
pointwise, then for each
y\in[0,1]
letting
x:=L_{1}^{-1}(y)
gives

L_{2}(x)\leq L_{1}(x)=y
, hence (since
L_{2}
is increasing)
L_{2}^{-1}(y)\geq x=L_{1}^{-1}(y)
. Thus
L_{1}^{-1}\leq L_{2}^{-1}
pointwise and

\mathcal{S}L_{1}\geq\mathcal{S}L_{2}
pointwise. If
\mathcal{S}L_{1}\geq\mathcal{S}L_{2}
pointwise, then applying
\mathcal{S}
again yields

L_{1}=\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{S}L_{1})\geq\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{S}L_{2})=L_{2}
. Hence
\mathcal{S}
is an order isomorphism
for the pointwise order on
\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}
.

(1.4) Sup-norm identity.

For
L_{1},L_{2}\in\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}
,

\|\mathcal{S}L_{1}-\mathcal{S}L_{2}\|_{\infty}=\sup_{u\in[0,1]}\bigl|L_{1}^{-1}(1-u)-L_{2}^{-1}(1-u)\bigr|=\|L_{1}^{-1}-L_{2}^{-1}\|_{\infty}.

(64)

(1.5) Continuity of inversion (hence of
\mathcal{S}
).

Let
f_{n},f\in C([0,1])
be strictly increasing with
f_{n}(0)=f(0)=0
,
f_{n}(1)=f(1)=1
, and
\|f_{n}-f\|_{\infty}\to 0
.
Fix
\varepsilon>0
and set

K_{\varepsilon}:=\{(x,y)\in[0,1]^{2}:\ |x-y|\geq\varepsilon\}.

The map
(x,y)\mapsto|f(x)-f(y)|
is continuous on the compact set
K_{\varepsilon}
, hence attains

m_{\varepsilon}:=\min_{(x,y)\in K_{\varepsilon}}|f(x)-f(y)|.

Strict monotonicity of
f
implies
m_{\varepsilon}>0
. Let
\eta:=m_{\varepsilon}/3
and take
n
large so that
\|f_{n}-f\|_{\infty}\leq\eta
.
Fix
u\in[0,1]
, set
x:=f^{-1}(u)
and
x_{n}:=f_{n}^{-1}(u)
. If
|x_{n}-x|\geq\varepsilon
, then
(x_{n},x)\in K_{\varepsilon}
, so

|f(x_{n})-f(x)|\geq m_{\varepsilon}.

But
f(x)=u=f_{n}(x_{n})
, hence

|f(x_{n})-f(x)|=|f(x_{n})-u|=|f(x_{n})-f_{n}(x_{n})|\leq\|f-f_{n}\|_{\infty}\leq\eta<m_{\varepsilon},

a contradiction. Therefore
|x_{n}-x|<\varepsilon
for all
u
, i.e.
\|f_{n}^{-1}-f^{-1}\|_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon
for all large
n
.
Thus
\|f_{n}^{-1}-f^{-1}\|_{\infty}\to 0
. Applying this to
f_{n}=L_{n}
shows
L\mapsto L^{-1}
is
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
-continuous on
\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}
,
hence
\mathcal{S}
is
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
-continuous on
\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}
by (
62
). Since
L\mapsto L^{-1}
is
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
–continuous on
\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}
and
\mathcal{S}
is built from inversion and affine maps,

\mathcal{S}
is
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
–continuous. Because
\mathcal{S}^{-1}=\mathcal{S}
, it is a homeomorphism of
\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}
.

Step 2: Mixture Conjugacy: Dual Mixtures
\leftrightarrow
Ordinary Mixtures.

For
L_{1},L_{3}\in\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}
and
\lambda\in(0,1)
define the dual mixture

L_{1}\oplus_{\lambda}L_{3}:=\bigl(\lambda L_{1}^{-1}+(1-\lambda)L_{3}^{-1}\bigr)^{-1}.

Since
L_{1}^{-1},L_{3}^{-1}
are strictly increasing concave maps with endpoints
(0,0)
and
(1,1)
,
their convex combination is strictly increasing concave, hence its inverse belongs to
\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}
.

For
u\in[0,1]
we compute

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(L_{1}\oplus_{\lambda}L_{3})(u)

\displaystyle=1-(L_{1}\oplus_{\lambda}L_{3})^{-1}(1-u)

\displaystyle=1-\bigl(\lambda L_{1}^{-1}(1-u)+(1-\lambda)L_{3}^{-1}(1-u)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\lambda\bigl(1-L_{1}^{-1}(1-u)\bigr)+(1-\lambda)\bigl(1-L_{3}^{-1}(1-u)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\lambda\,\mathcal{S}L_{1}(u)+(1-\lambda)\,\mathcal{S}L_{3}(u).

(65)

Applying
\mathcal{S}
to (
65
) and using
\mathcal{S}^{2}=\mathrm{id}
yields the
reverse
conjugacy:
for all
M_{1},M_{3}\in\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}
and
\lambda\in(0,1)
,

\mathcal{S}\bigl(\lambda M_{1}+(1-\lambda)M_{3}\bigr)=(\mathcal{S}M_{1})\oplus_{\lambda}(\mathcal{S}M_{3}).

(66)

Step 3: Transported Preorder and Affine Representation.

Define the transported preorder
\succsim^{\mathcal{S}}
on
\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}
by

M_{1}\succsim^{\mathcal{S}}M_{2}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\mathcal{S}M_{1}\succsim\mathcal{S}M_{2}.

Since
\mathcal{S}
is bijective (Step 1.2),
\succsim^{\mathcal{S}}
is a complete preorder.
Nontriviality transfers from (D4): if
L^{+}\succ L^{-}
then
\mathcal{S}L^{+}\succ^{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}L^{-}
.

(3.1) Mixture independence.

Fix
M_{1},M_{2},M_{3}\in\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}
and
\lambda\in(0,1)
, and set
L_{i}:=\mathcal{S}M_{i}
.
Then

\displaystyle M_{1}\succsim^{\mathcal{S}}M_{2}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow L_{1}\succsim L_{2}

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{(D1)}}{\Longleftrightarrow}L_{1}\oplus_{\lambda}L_{3}\succsim L_{2}\oplus_{\lambda}L_{3}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\mathcal{S}(L_{1}\oplus_{\lambda}L_{3})\succsim^{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}(L_{2}\oplus_{\lambda}L_{3})

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{eq:S-conjugacy-dual}}{\Longleftrightarrow}\bigl(\lambda M_{1}+(1-\lambda)M_{3}\bigr)\succsim^{\mathcal{S}}\bigl(\lambda M_{2}+(1-\lambda)M_{3}\bigr).

i.e.
\succsim^{\mathcal{S}}
satisfies ordinary mixture independence on
\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}
.

(3.2) Weak dominance.

If
M_{1}\geq M_{2}
pointwise, then
\mathcal{S}M_{1}\geq\mathcal{S}M_{2}
(Step 1.3),
hence
\mathcal{S}M_{1}\succsim\mathcal{S}M_{2}
by (D3), so
M_{1}\succsim^{\mathcal{S}}M_{2}
.

(3.3) Closed graph.

If
(M_{n},N_{n})\to(M,N)
in
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
and
M_{n}\succsim^{\mathcal{S}}N_{n}
for all
n
,
then
\mathcal{S}M_{n}\succsim\mathcal{S}N_{n}
for all
n
.
Since
\mathcal{S}
is a homeomorphism ,
(\mathcal{S}M_{n},\mathcal{S}N_{n})\to(\mathcal{S}M,\mathcal{S}N)
.
Closed graph of
\succsim
(D2) gives
\mathcal{S}M\succsim\mathcal{S}N
, hence
M\succsim^{\mathcal{S}}N
.

(3.4) Affine continuous representation.

By Proposition
1
applied to
(\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow},\succsim^{\mathcal{S}})
,
there exists a nonconstant affine
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
–continuous
W:\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}\to\mathbb{R}

such that

M_{1}\succsim^{\mathcal{S}}M_{2}\Longleftrightarrow W(M_{1})\geq W(M_{2}),\qquad W((1-\lambda)M_{1}+\lambda M_{2})=(1-\lambda)W(M_{1})+\lambda W(M_{2}).

Step 4: Dyadic Discrete Representation for
W
on
\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}
.

For each
m\in\mathbb{N}
set

n:=2^{m},\qquad t_{k}:=\frac{k}{n}\qquad(k=0,1,\dots,n).

Let

X_{m}:=\{g\in C([0,1]):g\text{ is affine on each }[t_{k-1},t_{k}]\},\qquad\mathcal{L}_{m}^{\uparrow}:=\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}\cap X_{m}.

Let
P_{m}:C([0,1])\to X_{m}
be polygonal interpolation:
(P_{m}f)(t_{k})=f(t_{k})
and
P_{m}f
affine on each interval.
Then
P_{m}f\to f
uniformly for all
f\in C([0,1])
, and
P_{m}M\in\mathcal{L}_{m}^{\uparrow}
for every
M\in\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}
.

Apply Theorem
5
with

\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow},

the preorder
\succsim^{\mathcal{S}}
, and the affine continuous representation
W
.
Then there exists
m_{0}
such that for every
m\geq m_{0}
there exists a finite nonnegative Borel measure

\nu_{m}

supported on
\{t_{1},\dots,t_{n-1}\}
such that

W(M)-W(N)=\int_{[0,1]}\bigl(M(u)-N(u)\bigr)\,\nu_{m}(du)\qquad(\forall M,N\in\mathcal{L}_{m}^{\uparrow}).

(67)

Define

p_{m}(u):=\nu_{m}((u,1])\qquad(0\leq u<1),\qquad p_{m}(1):=0.

Then
p_{m}
is a nonincreasing right–continuous step function on the dyadic grid.
Writing

p_{m}|_{[t_{k-1},t_{k})}\equiv p(m,k)\qquad(k=1,\dots,n),

we have

p(m,n)=0.

Because
\nu_{m}
is supported on
\{t_{1},\dots,t_{n-1}\}
,

p(m,k)=\sum_{j=k}^{n-1}\nu_{m}(\{t_{j}\}),\qquad\nu_{m}(\{t_{k}\})=p(m,k)-p(m,k+1)\qquad(k=1,\dots,n-1).

In particular,

\nu_{m}([0,1])=p(m,1)\geq 0,\qquad 0\leq p_{m}(u)\leq p(m,1)\qquad(u\in[0,1]).

If
M\in\mathcal{L}_{m}^{\uparrow}
, then discrete integration by parts gives

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}p_{m}\,dM

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}p(m,k)\bigl(M(t_{k})-M(t_{k-1})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}M(t_{k})\bigl(p(m,k)-p(m,k+1)\bigr)+M(1)p(m,n)-M(0)p(m,1)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}M(t_{k})\,\nu_{m}(\{t_{k}\})

\displaystyle=\int_{[0,1]}M(u)\,\nu_{m}(du),

(68)

using
M(0)=0
,
M(1)=1
, and
p(m,n)=0
.

Let

E(u):=u.

Since
E\in\mathcal{L}_{m}^{\uparrow}
, define

c_{m}:=W(E)-\int_{[0,1]}E(u)\,\nu_{m}(du).

Taking
N=E
in (
67
) yields

W(M)-W(E)=\int_{[0,1]}\bigl(M(u)-E(u)\bigr)\,\nu_{m}(du),

hence

W(M)-c_{m}=\int_{[0,1]}M(u)\,\nu_{m}(du).

Combining this with (
68
), we obtain

W(M)-c_{m}=\int_{0}^{1}p_{m}(u)\,dM(u)=\int_{[0,1]}M(u)\,\nu_{m}(du)\qquad(\forall M\in\mathcal{L}_{m}^{\uparrow}).

(69)

Step 5: Uniform Mass bound from Archimedean continuity for
\succsim^{\mathcal{S}}
.

Assume (D5). Let
B^{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}
and
C,\delta>0
be given by
Axiom
7
for the transported preorder
\succsim^{\mathcal{S}}
, and set

\Delta_{B^{\mathcal{S}}}:=W(E)-W(B^{\mathcal{S}})>0.

(5.1) Global Bound.

We claim that

W(E)-W(M)\leq C\Delta_{B^{\mathcal{S}}}\,\|E-M\|_{\infty}\qquad(M\in\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}).

Indeed, if
0<\|E-M\|_{\infty}<\delta
, then by Axiom
7
there exists

s_{M}\in[0,1]
such that

s_{M}\leq C\|E-M\|_{\infty}\qquad\text{and}\qquad M\succsim^{\mathcal{S}}(1-s_{M})E+s_{M}B^{\mathcal{S}}.

Applying the affine representation
W
gives

W(M)\geq(1-s_{M})W(E)+s_{M}W(B^{\mathcal{S}})=W(E)-s_{M}\Delta_{B^{\mathcal{S}}},

hence

W(E)-W(M)\leq s_{M}\Delta_{B^{\mathcal{S}}}\leq C\Delta_{B^{\mathcal{S}}}\,\|E-M\|_{\infty}.

Now let
M\in\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}
be arbitrary. If
M=E
, there is nothing to prove. Otherwise define

\theta:=\min\Bigl\{1,\frac{\delta}{2\|E-M\|_{\infty}}\Bigr\}\in(0,1],\qquad M^{\theta}:=(1-\theta)E+\theta M.

Then

\|E-M^{\theta}\|_{\infty}=\theta\|E-M\|_{\infty}<\delta,

so the previous estimate applied to
M^{\theta}
yields

W(E)-W(M^{\theta})\leq C\Delta_{B^{\mathcal{S}}}\,\|E-M^{\theta}\|_{\infty}=C\Delta_{B^{\mathcal{S}}}\,\theta\|E-M\|_{\infty}.

Since

W(M^{\theta})=(1-\theta)W(E)+\theta W(M),

we have

W(E)-W(M^{\theta})=\theta\bigl(W(E)-W(M)\bigr).

Cancelling
\theta>0
yields

W(E)-W(M)\leq C\Delta_{B^{\mathcal{S}}}\,\|E-M\|_{\infty}\qquad(M\in\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}).

(5.2) Strict corner curves (stay inside
\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}
).

Fix once and for all some
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. For
j\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
define

K_{m,j}(u):=\max\Bigl\{0,\frac{u-t_{j}}{1-t_{j}}\Bigr\}\in X_{m},

and define the strictified corners

K_{m,1}^{\varepsilon}:=(1-\varepsilon)K_{m,1}+\varepsilon E,\qquad K_{m,n-1}^{\varepsilon}:=(1-\varepsilon)K_{m,n-1}+\varepsilon E.

Then
K_{m,1}^{\varepsilon},K_{m,n-1}^{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{L}_{m}^{\uparrow}
.

(5.3) A clean mass identity.

Let
S_{m}:=\sum_{k=1}^{n}p(m,k)
, so
\int_{0}^{1}p_{m}\,dE=\frac{1}{n}S_{m}
.
Since
p(m,n)=0
, one checks from dyadic increments that

\int_{0}^{1}p_{m}\,dK_{m,n-1}^{\varepsilon}=\frac{\varepsilon}{n}S_{m},\qquad\int_{0}^{1}p_{m}\,dK_{m,1}^{\varepsilon}=\frac{\varepsilon}{n}p(m,1)+\Bigl(\frac{1-\varepsilon}{n-1}+\frac{\varepsilon}{n}\Bigr)\sum_{k=2}^{n}p(m,k).

Using (
69
) for
E,K_{m,1}^{\varepsilon},K_{m,n-1}^{\varepsilon}
and eliminating
c_{m}
gives

\nu_{m}([0,1])=p(m,1)=\frac{\bigl(W(E)-W(K_{m,n-1}^{\varepsilon})\bigr)+(n-1)\bigl(W(E)-W(K_{m,1}^{\varepsilon})\bigr)}{1-\varepsilon}.

(70)

(5.4) Uniform bound.

Since

\|E-K_{m,1}^{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}=(1-\varepsilon)\|E-K_{m,1}\|_{\infty}=\frac{1-\varepsilon}{n},

and

\|E-K_{m,n-1}^{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}=(1-\varepsilon)\|E-K_{m,n-1}\|_{\infty}=(1-\varepsilon)\frac{n-1}{n}\leq 1-\varepsilon,

the global estimate
(\star)
yields

W(E)-W(K_{m,1}^{\varepsilon})\leq C\Delta_{B^{\mathcal{S}}}\,\frac{1-\varepsilon}{n},

and

W(E)-W(K_{m,n-1}^{\varepsilon})\leq C\Delta_{B^{\mathcal{S}}}(1-\varepsilon)\frac{n-1}{n}.

Substituting these bounds into (
70
) gives

\nu_{m}([0,1])\leq\frac{C\Delta_{B^{\mathcal{S}}}(1-\varepsilon)\frac{n-1}{n}+(n-1)C\Delta_{B^{\mathcal{S}}}(1-\varepsilon)\frac{1}{n}}{1-\varepsilon}\leq 2C\Delta_{B^{\mathcal{S}}}.

Thus

0\leq\nu_{m}([0,1])\leq 2C\Delta_{B^{\mathcal{S}}}\qquad(\forall m\geq m_{0}).

(71)

Since

c_{m}=W(E)-\int_{[0,1]}E(u)\,\nu_{m}(du),

the same bound implies

W(E)-2C\Delta_{B^{\mathcal{S}}}\leq c_{m}\leq W(E),

so
(c_{m})
is bounded.

Step 6: Limit Measure for
W
on
\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}
.

For
m\geq m_{0}
define
T_{m}:C([0,1])\to\mathbb{R}
by
T_{m}(f):=\int_{[0,1]}f\,d\nu_{m}
.
Then
T_{m}
is positive and

\|T_{m}\|=T_{m}(\mathbf{1})=\nu_{m}([0,1])\leq M_{0}

by (
71
).

Let
\mathscr{D}\subset C([0,1])
be the countable
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
–dense dyadic–rational polygonal class
(from the primal theorem’s density step), enumerate
\mathscr{D}=\{d_{1},d_{2},\dots\}
.
By diagonal selection choose a strictly increasing subsequence
(m_{j})
such that

T_{m_{j}}(d_{r})
converges for every
r
.
Since
(c_{m})
is bounded, pass to a further subsequence (not relabeled) such that
c_{m_{j}}\to c\in\mathbb{R}
.

Define
T(f):=\lim_{j\to\infty}T_{m_{j}}(f)
for
f\in C([0,1])
.
The uniform bound
\|T_{m_{j}}\|\leq M_{0}
and density of
\mathscr{D}
imply this limit exists for all
f
,
and
T
is a positive continuous linear functional with
\|T\|\leq M_{0}
.
By Riesz–Markov, there exists a finite nonnegative Borel measure
\nu
on
[0,1]
such that

T(f)=\int_{[0,1]}f\,d\nu\qquad(\forall f\in C([0,1])).

Finally fix
M\in\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}
and set
M^{(j)}:=P_{m_{j}}M\in\mathcal{L}_{m_{j}}^{\uparrow}
.
Then
\|M^{(j)}-M\|_{\infty}\to 0
and continuity of
W
gives
W(M^{(j)})\to W(M)
.
Using (
69
) at
m=m_{j}
,

W(M^{(j)})-c_{m_{j}}=T_{m_{j}}(M^{(j)}).

Moreover,

T_{m_{j}}(M^{(j)})-T(M)=T_{m_{j}}(M^{(j)}-M)+\bigl(T_{m_{j}}(M)-T(M)\bigr),

and
|T_{m_{j}}(M^{(j)}-M)|\leq\|T_{m_{j}}\|\,\|M^{(j)}-M\|_{\infty}\leq M_{0}\|M^{(j)}-M\|_{\infty}\to 0
,
while
T_{m_{j}}(M)\to T(M)
by definition of
T
.
Hence
T_{m_{j}}(M^{(j)})\to T(M)
and letting
j\to\infty
yields

W(M)-c=\int_{[0,1]}M\,d\nu\qquad(\forall M\in\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}).

(72)

Step 7: Pullback, Cumulative kernel, and Measure form.

Define

U(L):=W(\mathcal{S}L)\qquad(L\in\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}).

Then
U
represents
\succsim
on
\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}
.

Let
\mu_{0}
denote the finite nonnegative Borel measure obtained in Step 6, so that

W(M)=c+\int_{[0,1]}M(u)\,\mu_{0}(du)\qquad(M\in\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}).

Hence, for
L\in\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}
,

\displaystyle U(L)

\displaystyle=W(\mathcal{S}L)

\displaystyle=c+\int_{[0,1]}\bigl(1-L^{-1}(1-u)\bigr)\,\mu_{0}(du)

\displaystyle=c+\mu_{0}([0,1])-\int_{[0,1]}L^{-1}(1-u)\,\mu_{0}(du).

Let
\tau:[0,1]\to[0,1]
be given by
\tau(u):=1-u
, and define the pushforward measure

\nu:=\mu_{0}\circ\tau^{-1}.

Then

\int_{[0,1]}L^{-1}(1-u)\,\mu_{0}(du)=\int_{[0,1]}L^{-1}(v)\,\nu(dv),

so

U(L)=c+\nu([0,1])-\int_{[0,1]}L^{-1}(v)\,\nu(dv)\qquad(L\in\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}).

(73)

Define
q:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
by

q(u):=\nu([0,u])\qquad(0\leq u<1),\qquad q(1):=q(1^{-}),

where the left limit exists because the map
u\mapsto\nu([0,u])
is nondecreasing on
[0,1)
.

By continuity from below of the finite measure
\nu
,

q(1)=\lim_{t\uparrow 1}\nu([0,t])=\nu\!\left(\bigcup_{t<1}[0,t]\right)=\nu([0,1)).

For
0\leq u<1
,

q(u)=\nu([0,u])\leq\nu([0,1))=q(1).

Hence
q
is nondecreasing on
[0,1]
.

Let
u\in[0,1)
and let
u_{n}\downarrow u
. Then
[0,u_{n}]\downarrow[0,u]
, so continuity from above gives

q(u_{n})=\nu([0,u_{n}])\longrightarrow\nu([0,u])=q(u).

Thus
q
is right–continuous on
[0,1)
. Since
q
is monotone, it belongs to
BV([0,1])
.

Define the auxiliary function

\widehat{q}(u):=\nu([0,u])\qquad(u\in[0,1]).

Then
\widehat{q}(u)=q(u)
for
u\in[0,1)
, while

\widehat{q}(1)=\nu([0,1]).

Hence
q-\widehat{q}
vanishes on
[0,1)
.

We claim that

\int_{0}^{1}q(u)\,d\bigl(L^{-1}(u)\bigr)=\int_{0}^{1}\widehat{q}(u)\,d\bigl(L^{-1}(u)\bigr)\qquad(\forall L\in\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}).

Indeed, let
s:=q-\widehat{q}
. Then
s(u)=0
for
u\in[0,1)
, and

s(1)=q(1)-\widehat{q}(1)=\nu([0,1))-\nu([0,1])=-\nu(\{1\}).

Since
s
differs from the zero function at only one point,
s\in BV([0,1])
. Fix
L\in\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}
and
\varepsilon>0
.
By continuity of
L^{-1}
at
1
, choose
\delta\in(0,1)
such that

0\leq L^{-1}(1)-L^{-1}(1-\delta)<\varepsilon.

Let
P:0=t_{0}<\cdots<t_{m}=1
be any partition containing
1-\delta
, and let
\xi_{k}\in[t_{k-1},t_{k}]
.
Then the Riemann–Stieltjes sum

S(P,\xi;s,L^{-1}):=\sum_{k=1}^{m}s(\xi_{k})\bigl(L^{-1}(t_{k})-L^{-1}(t_{k-1})\bigr)

has at most one nonzero term, and therefore

|S(P,\xi;s,L^{-1})|\leq|s(1)|\bigl(L^{-1}(1)-L^{-1}(1-\delta)\bigr)<|s(1)|\,\varepsilon.

Letting the mesh tend to
0
yields

\int_{0}^{1}s\,d(L^{-1})=0,

which proves the claim.

Since
\nu
is the Lebesgue–Stieltjes measure induced by
\widehat{q}
, one has

\int_{0}^{1}L^{-1}(u)\,d\widehat{q}(u)=\int_{[0,1]}L^{-1}(u)\,\nu(du).

Now fix
L\in\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}
. Since
L^{-1}
is continuous, increasing, and of bounded variation,
the integration–by–parts formula gives

\int_{0}^{1}\widehat{q}(u)\,d\bigl(L^{-1}(u)\bigr)+\int_{0}^{1}L^{-1}(u)\,d\widehat{q}(u)=\widehat{q}(1)L^{-1}(1)-\widehat{q}(0)L^{-1}(0).

Because
\widehat{q}(1)=\nu([0,1])
,
\widehat{q}(0)=\nu([0,0])
, and

L^{-1}(0)=0,\qquad L^{-1}(1)=1,

we obtain

\int_{0}^{1}\widehat{q}(u)\,d\bigl(L^{-1}(u)\bigr)=\nu([0,1])-\int_{[0,1]}L^{-1}(u)\,\nu(du).

Therefore

\int_{0}^{1}q(u)\,d\bigl(L^{-1}(u)\bigr)=\nu([0,1])-\int_{[0,1]}L^{-1}(u)\,\nu(du).

Combining this with (
73
), we obtain

U(L)=c+\int_{0}^{1}q(u)\,d\bigl(L^{-1}(u)\bigr)\qquad(L\in\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}).

Equivalently, setting

c^{\prime}:=c+\nu([0,1]),

we have

U(L)=c^{\prime}-\int_{[0,1]}L^{-1}(u)\,\nu(du)\qquad(L\in\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}).

This proves the existence part.

Step 8: Uniqueness up to Positive Affine Transformations.

Let
\widetilde{c}\in\mathbb{R}
and let
\widetilde{q}:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
be another nondecreasing bounded–variation kernel,
right–continuous on
[0,1)
, satisfying

\widetilde{q}(1)=\widetilde{q}(1^{-})
, and such that

\widetilde{V}(L):=\widetilde{c}+\int_{0}^{1}\widetilde{q}(u)\,d\bigl(L^{-1}(u)\bigr)\qquad(L\in\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow})

also represents
\succsim
on
\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}
.

Set

q_{1}:=q,\qquad q_{2}:=\widetilde{q},\qquad c_{1}:=c,\qquad c_{2}:=\widetilde{c}.

For
i=1,2
, define

V_{i}(L):=c_{i}+\int_{0}^{1}q_{i}(u)\,d(L^{-1})(u)\qquad(L\in\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}).

For
L_{1},L_{2}\in\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}
and
\lambda\in[0,1]
, the definition of the dual mixture gives

(L_{1}\oplus_{\lambda}L_{2})^{-1}=\lambda L_{1}^{-1}+(1-\lambda)L_{2}^{-1}.

Hence

\displaystyle V_{i}(L_{1}\oplus_{\lambda}L_{2})

\displaystyle=c_{i}+\int_{0}^{1}q_{i}(u)\,d\bigl((L_{1}\oplus_{\lambda}L_{2})^{-1}(u)\bigr)

\displaystyle=c_{i}+\int_{0}^{1}q_{i}(u)\,d\bigl(\lambda L_{1}^{-1}(u)+(1-\lambda)L_{2}^{-1}(u)\bigr)

\displaystyle=c_{i}+\lambda\int_{0}^{1}q_{i}(u)\,dL_{1}^{-1}(u)+(1-\lambda)\int_{0}^{1}q_{i}(u)\,dL_{2}^{-1}(u)

\displaystyle=\lambda V_{i}(L_{1})+(1-\lambda)V_{i}(L_{2}).

Thus each
V_{i}
is affine with respect to dual mixtures.

Define

\Phi_{i}(M):=V_{i}(\mathcal{S}M)\qquad(M\in\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow},\ i=1,2).

Then each
\Phi_{i}
represents
\succsim^{\mathcal{S}}
. Moreover, for
M_{1},M_{2}\in\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}
and
\lambda\in[0,1]
,

\mathcal{S}\bigl(\lambda M_{1}+(1-\lambda)M_{2}\bigr)=(\mathcal{S}M_{1})\oplus_{\lambda}(\mathcal{S}M_{2})

by (
66
). Therefore

\displaystyle\Phi_{i}\bigl(\lambda M_{1}+(1-\lambda)M_{2}\bigr)

\displaystyle=V_{i}\bigl(\mathcal{S}(\lambda M_{1}+(1-\lambda)M_{2})\bigr)

\displaystyle=V_{i}\bigl((\mathcal{S}M_{1})\oplus_{\lambda}(\mathcal{S}M_{2})\bigr)
