G^{\prime}(x)-G^{\prime}(c)\leq G^{\prime}(x_{0})-G^{\prime}(c)\quad\Longrightarrow\quad G^{\prime}(x)\leq G^{\prime}(x_{0})\quad(x\in C).

Hence For every convex
C\subset X
with
\operatorname{int}C\neq\varnothing
and every
x_{0}\notin C
, there exists a nonzero continuous linear functional
G:X\to\mathbb{R}
such that
G(x)\leq G(x_{0})
for all
x\in C
(take
G=G^{\prime}
above).

Now let
A,B\subset X
be nonempty convex sets with
A\cap B=\varnothing
and
\operatorname{int}A\neq\varnothing
. Define

Z:=B-A:=\{b-a:b\in B,\ a\in A\}.

Then
Z
is convex, being the image of
B\times A
under the linear map
(b,a)\mapsto b-a
. If
0\in Z
, then
0=b-a
for some
a\in A
,
b\in B
, so
a=b\in A\cap B
, a contradiction; hence
0\notin Z
.

We show
\operatorname{int}Z\neq\varnothing
. Choose
a_{0}\in\operatorname{int}A
and
b_{0}\in B
. There exists
r>0
with
B(a_{0},r)\subset A
. For any
x\in X
with
|x|<r
, one has
a_{0}+x\in A
, so

(b_{0}-a_{0})-x=b_{0}-(a_{0}+x)\in Z.

Thus
B(b_{0}-a_{0},r)\subset Z
, hence
b_{0}-a_{0}\in\operatorname{int}Z
and
\operatorname{int}Z\neq\varnothing
.

Apply (*) to
C:=Z
and
x_{0}:=0
. There exists a nonzero continuous linear functional
G:X\to\mathbb{R}
such that

G(z)\leq G(0)=0\quad\text{for all }z\in Z.

For
a\in A
and
b\in B
we have
z:=b-a\in Z
, hence

G(b)-G(a)=G(b-a)\leq 0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad G(a)\geq G(b)\quad(a\in A,\ b\in B).

Define

\alpha:=\inf_{a\in A}G(a),\qquad\beta:=\sup_{b\in B}G(b).

Since
A,B
are nonempty, choose
a_{0}\in A
,
b_{0}\in B
. From
G(b)\leq G(a_{0})
for all
b\in B
we obtain
\beta\leq G(a_{0})<\infty
, and from
G(a)\geq G(b_{0})
for all
a\in A
we obtain
\alpha\geq G(b_{0})>-\infty
. Moreover
G(a)\geq G(b)
for all
a\in A
,
b\in B
implies
\alpha\geq\beta
. Thus
[\beta,\alpha]
is a nonempty interval; choose any
\gamma\in[\beta,\alpha]
. Then, for all
a\in A
and
b\in B
,

G(a)\geq\alpha\geq\gamma\geq\beta\geq G(b),

so, with
F:=G
,

F(a)\geq\gamma\geq F(b)\quad(\forall a\in A,\ \forall b\in B),

and
F
is a nonzero continuous linear functional on
X
. This is the separation that would be useful to us for∎

Proof A.8  (Proof of
Lemma

7
)

Back to
Lemma

7
.

Proof.

For
0\leq s\leq t<1
we have
\mathcal{L}_{s}\subset\mathcal{L}_{t}
(if
\phi=0
on
[s,1]
then
\phi=0
on
[t,1]
), hence

\alpha(s)\leq\alpha(t)\qquad(0\leq s\leq t<1),

so
\alpha
is increasing on
[0,1)
.

If
t=0
then
\mathcal{L}_{0}=\{0\}
, hence
\alpha(0)=L(0)=0
.
If
t=1
then
\mathcal{L}_{1}=\{\phi\in X:0\leq\phi\leq 1\}
.
For every
\phi\in\mathcal{L}_{1}
we have
0\leq\phi\leq\mathbf{1}
, hence
0\leq L(\phi)\leq L(\mathbf{1})
by positivity.
Also
\mathbf{1}\in\mathcal{L}_{1}
, hence

\alpha(1)=L(\mathbf{1}).

Thus
\alpha
is increasing on all of
[0,1]
and we have the boundary values:

\alpha(0)=0,\qquad\alpha(1)=L(\mathbf{1}),\qquad 0\leq\alpha(t)\leq L(\mathbf{1})\ \ (t\in[0,1]).

For
a\in[0,1)
define the right limit

\alpha(a+):=\lim_{h\downarrow 0}\alpha(a+h)=\inf_{\delta>0}\alpha(a+\delta).

If
L(\mathbf{1})=0
, then for any
f\in X
with
f\geq 0
,

0\leq f\leq\|f\|_{\infty}\,\mathbf{1}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad 0\leq L(f)\leq\|f\|_{\infty}\,L(\mathbf{1})=0,

so
L(f)=0
for all
f\geq 0
, hence
L\equiv 0
on
X
by linearity.
Also
\alpha\equiv 0
. Then
L(f)=0=\int_{0}^{1}f\,d\alpha
for all
f
.
Henceforth assume
L(\mathbf{1})>0
.

We are going to use moving window class of bridging increasing functions to establish the global properties of the unit interval.
For
0\leq a<b\leq 1
define

\mathcal{B}_{(a,b]}:=\bigl\{\psi\in X:\ 0\leq\psi\leq 1,\ \psi(x)=0\ \text{for all }x\in[0,a]\cup(b,1]\bigr\}.

(If
b<1
, continuity implies
\psi(b)=0
because
\psi\equiv 0
on
(b,1]
.)

Now we establish the identity that for every
0\leq a<b\leq 1
:

\sup_{\psi\in\mathcal{B}_{(a,b]}}L(\psi)\ =\ \alpha(b)-\alpha(a+).

(115)

Proof of (
115
), “
\leq
” direction.

Fix
0\leq a<b\leq 1
and
\psi\in\mathcal{B}_{(a,b]}
. Then
\psi(a)=0
and
\psi
is continuous.
Choose arbitrary
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\delta\in(0,b-a)
such that

0\leq\psi(x)\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2L(\mathbf{1})}\qquad\forall x\in[a,a+\delta].

Moreover, there exists
\phi_{0}\in\mathcal{L}_{a+\delta}
such that

L(\phi_{0})\ \geq\ \alpha(a+\delta)-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}.

Define

\phi(x):=\min\{\phi_{0}(x),\,1-\psi(x)\}\qquad(x\in[0,1]).

Then
\phi\in X
,
0\leq\phi\leq 1
.
Also
\phi\leq\phi_{0}
and
\phi_{0}=0
on
[a+\delta,1]
, hence
\phi=0
on
[a+\delta,1]
.
Therefore
\phi\in\mathcal{L}_{a+\delta}
.

We now establish
\phi+\psi\in\mathcal{L}_{b}
. Since
\phi\leq 1-\psi
, we have
0\leq\phi+\psi\leq 1
.
If
b<1
, then
\phi=0
on
[a+\delta,1]\supset[b,1]
and
\psi=0
on
(b,1]
; by continuity
\psi(b)=0
,
so
\psi=0
on
[b,1]
and therefore
\phi+\psi=0
on
[b,1]
, i.e.
\phi+\psi\in\mathcal{L}_{b}
.
If
b=1
, then
\phi+\psi\in\mathcal{L}_{1}
because
0\leq\phi+\psi\leq 1
.
In both cases,

L(\phi+\psi)\leq\alpha(b).

By linearity,

L(\psi)\leq\alpha(b)-L(\phi).

Next we establish the lower bound on
L(\phi)
. Let
d:=\phi_{0}-\phi\geq 0
. For each
x
:
if
\phi_{0}(x)\leq 1-\psi(x)
then
\phi(x)=\phi_{0}(x)
so
d(x)=0
;
if
\phi_{0}(x)>1-\psi(x)
then
\phi(x)=1-\psi(x)
so

d(x)=\phi_{0}(x)-(1-\psi(x))\leq 1-(1-\psi(x))=\psi(x),

using
\phi_{0}(x)\leq 1
. Hence

0\leq d\leq\psi\quad\text{pointwise on }[0,1].

(116)

Moreover,
\phi_{0}=0
on
[a+\delta,1]
so
d=0
there; and
\psi=0
on
[0,a]
so
d=0
there by (
116
).
Thus
\supp(d)\subset[a,a+\delta]
, and on that interval
\psi\leq\varepsilon/(2L(\mathbf{1}))
, so by (
116
),

0\leq d\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2L(\mathbf{1})}\,\mathbf{1}.

By positivity,

0\leq L(d)\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2L(\mathbf{1})}\,L(\mathbf{1})=\frac{\varepsilon}{2}.

Therefore

L(\phi)=L(\phi_{0})-L(d)\ \geq\ \Bigl(\alpha(a+\delta)-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\Bigr)-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}=\alpha(a+\delta)-\varepsilon.

Insert into
(\star)
:

L(\psi)\leq\alpha(b)-\alpha(a+\delta)+\varepsilon\leq\alpha(b)-\alpha(a+)+\varepsilon,

since
\alpha(a+)=\inf_{\delta>0}\alpha(a+\delta)\leq\alpha(a+\delta)
.
Let
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
to obtain

L(\psi)\leq\alpha(b)-\alpha(a+).

Taking the supremum over
\psi\in\mathcal{B}_{(a,b]}
yields

\sup_{\psi\in\mathcal{B}_{(a,b]}}L(\psi)\ \leq\ \alpha(b)-\alpha(a+).

Proof of (
115
), “
\geq
” direction.

Fix
0\leq a<b\leq 1
and
\varepsilon>0
.
Choose
\phi_{b}\in\mathcal{L}_{b}
such that

L(\phi_{b})>\alpha(b)-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}.

Choose
\delta\in(0,b-a)
such that

\alpha(a+\delta)\leq\alpha(a+)+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}.

Define the cutoff

\chi_{a,\delta}(x):=\begin{cases}1,&0\leq x\leq a,\\[2.0pt]
1-\dfrac{x-a}{\delta},&a<x<a+\delta,\\[6.0pt]
0,&x\geq a+\delta.\end{cases}

Then
0\leq\chi_{a,\delta}\leq 1
and
\chi_{a,\delta}=0
on
[a+\delta,1]
, hence
\chi_{a,\delta}\in\mathcal{L}_{a+\delta}
.
Define

\psi:=\phi_{b}(1-\chi_{a,\delta}).

Then
0\leq\psi\leq 1
, and
\psi=0
on
[0,a]
because
1-\chi_{a,\delta}=0
there.
If
b<1
, then
\phi_{b}=0
on
[b,1]
, hence
\psi=0
on
(b,1]
; if
b=1
, there is no constraint on
(b,1]
.
Thus
\psi\in\mathcal{B}_{(a,b]}
.

Also
\phi_{b}\chi_{a,\delta}\in\mathcal{L}_{a+\delta}
, so

L(\phi_{b}\chi_{a,\delta})\leq\alpha(a+\delta).

By linearity,

L(\psi)=L(\phi_{b})-L(\phi_{b}\chi_{a,\delta})\geq\Bigl(\alpha(b)-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\Bigr)-\alpha(a+\delta)\geq\alpha(b)-\alpha(a+)-\varepsilon.

Taking the supremum over
\psi\in\mathcal{B}_{(a,b]}
and letting
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
yields

\sup_{\psi\in\mathcal{B}_{(a,b]}}L(\psi)\ \geq\ \alpha(b)-\alpha(a+).

Together with Step 3 this proves (
115
).

We are back in the arena of classical Riemann–Stieltjes integral.
Fix
f\in X
. For a partition
P:0=t_{0}<\dots<t_{m}=1
define

m_{i}:=\inf_{[t_{i-1},t_{i}]}f,\qquad M_{i}:=\sup_{[t_{i-1},t_{i}]}f,

and the Darboux–Stieltjes sums

L(P;f,\alpha):=\sum_{i=1}^{m}m_{i}\bigl(\alpha(t_{i})-\alpha(t_{i-1})\bigr),\qquad U(P;f,\alpha):=\sum_{i=1}^{m}M_{i}\bigl(\alpha(t_{i})-\alpha(t_{i-1})\bigr).

Let
\omega_{f}(\delta):=\sup\{|f(x)-f(y)|:\ |x-y|\leq\delta\}
be the modulus of continuity of
f
.
For mesh
\|P\|:=\max_{i}(t_{i}-t_{i-1})
we have
M_{i}-m_{i}\leq\omega_{f}(\|P\|)
, hence

U(P;f,\alpha)-L(P;f,\alpha)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}(M_{i}-m_{i})\bigl(\alpha(t_{i})-\alpha(t_{i-1})\bigr)\leq\omega_{f}(\|P\|)\sum_{i=1}^{m}\bigl(\alpha(t_{i})-\alpha(t_{i-1})\bigr)=\omega_{f}(\|P\|)\bigl(\alpha(1)-\alpha(0)\bigr).

Since
f
is uniformly continuous,
\omega_{f}(\delta)\to 0
as
\delta\downarrow 0
.
Thus for every
\varepsilon>0
there exists a partition
P
with
U(P;f,\alpha)-L(P;f,\alpha)<\varepsilon
.
Consequently

\sup_{P}L(P;f,\alpha)=\inf_{P}U(P;f,\alpha)=:\int_{0}^{1}f\,d\alpha,

so the Riemann–Stieltjes integral exists for every
f\in X
.

We are going to pin down the Representation for
f\geq 0
. Let’s Fix
f\in X
with
f\geq 0
. Set
B:=\|f\|_{\infty}
.
Define the right-jump function

J(x):=\alpha(x^{+})-\alpha(x)\geq 0,\qquad x\in[0,1),

and the jump set
D:=\{x\in[0,1):J(x)>0\}
.

Claim 1:
D
is at most countable and
\sum_{x\in D}J(x)<\infty
.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
set
D_{n}:=\{x\in[0,1):J(x)\geq 1/n\}
.
If
x_{1}<\dots<x_{k}
are distinct points in
D_{n}
, choose
h>0
so small that
x_{k}+h\leq 1
and the intervals
[x_{j},x_{j}+h]
are disjoint.
Since
\alpha
is increasing,

J(x_{j})=\alpha(x_{j}^{+})-\alpha(x_{j})\leq\alpha(x_{j}+h)-\alpha(x_{j}).

Define the increasing sequence

r_{0}:=0,\ r_{1}:=x_{1},\ r_{2}:=x_{1}+h,\ r_{3}:=x_{2},\ r_{4}:=x_{2}+h,\ \dots,\ r_{2k-1}:=x_{k},\ r_{2k}:=x_{k}+h,\ r_{2k+1}:=1.

Then

\alpha(1)-\alpha(0)=\sum_{\ell=1}^{2k+1}\bigl(\alpha(r_{\ell})-\alpha(r_{\ell-1})\bigr)\geq\sum_{j=1}^{k}\bigl(\alpha(x_{j}+h)-\alpha(x_{j})\bigr)\geq\sum_{j=1}^{k}J(x_{j}).

But each
J(x_{j})\geq 1/n
, hence
k/n\leq\alpha(1)-\alpha(0)=L(\mathbf{1})
, so
k<\infty
.
Thus each
D_{n}
is finite, hence
D=\bigcup_{n\geq 1}D_{n}
is countable.
Enumerate
D=\{d_{1},d_{2},\dots\}
(finite or countably infinite) and set
J_{k}:=J(d_{k})
.
By the same finite-set estimate above, the partial sums
\sum_{k=1}^{N}J_{k}
are bounded by
L(\mathbf{1})
, hence

\sum_{k\geq 1}J_{k}<\infty
.

Fix
\varepsilon>0
.
Choose
N
such that

\sum_{k>N}J_{k}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{B+1},

and set
F:=\{d_{1},\dots,d_{N}\}
(possibly empty).

Claim 2: there exists a partition
P:0=t_{0}<\dots<t_{m}=1
such that

\|P\|\ \text{is arbitrarily small, and}\quad\{t_{1},\dots,t_{m-1}\}\cap F=\emptyset.

(Construction: choose
t_{1}\in(0,\delta]\setminus F
, then
t_{2}\in(t_{1},t_{1}+\delta]\setminus F
, etc., until reaching
1
;
this is possible since
F
is finite.)

Choose such a partition
P
with
\|P\|
so small that

U(P;f,\alpha)-L(P;f,\alpha)<\varepsilon.

(117)

Lower bound on
L(f)
.

Fix
\eta>0
. Choose
\phi_{1}\in\mathcal{L}_{t_{1}}
such that

L(\phi_{1})\geq\alpha(t_{1})-\eta.

For each
i=2,\dots,m
, use (
115
) to choose
\psi_{i}\in\mathcal{B}_{(t_{i-1},t_{i}]}
such that

L(\psi_{i})\geq\bigl(\alpha(t_{i})-\alpha(t_{i-1}+)\bigr)-\eta.

Define

\Psi:=m_{1}\,\phi_{1}+\sum_{i=2}^{m}m_{i}\,\psi_{i}\in X.

Then
0\leq\Psi\leq f
pointwise, hence by positivity,

L(f)\geq L(\Psi).

By linearity,

L(\Psi)\geq m_{1}\bigl(\alpha(t_{1})-\eta\bigr)+\sum_{i=2}^{m}m_{i}\Bigl(\alpha(t_{i})-\alpha(t_{i-1}+)-\eta\Bigr).

Thus

L(f)\geq\sum_{i=1}^{m}m_{i}\bigl(\alpha(t_{i})-\alpha(t_{i-1})\bigr)-\sum_{i=2}^{m}m_{i}\bigl(\alpha(t_{i-1}+)-\alpha(t_{i-1})\bigr)-\eta\sum_{i=1}^{m}m_{i}.

Since
0\leq m_{i}\leq B
and
\{t_{1},\dots,t_{m-1}\}\cap F=\emptyset
,

\sum_{i=2}^{m}m_{i}\bigl(\alpha(t_{i-1}+)-\alpha(t_{i-1})\bigr)=\sum_{i=2}^{m}m_{i}\,J(t_{i-1})\leq B\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=2,\dots,m\\
t_{i-1}\in D\setminus F\end{subarray}}J(t_{i-1})\leq B\sum_{k>N}J_{k}\leq B\cdot\frac{\varepsilon}{B+1}<\varepsilon.

Choose
\eta>0
so small that
mB\eta<\varepsilon
. Then
\eta\sum_{i=1}^{m}m_{i}\leq\eta\,mB<\varepsilon
, hence

L(f)\geq L(P;f,\alpha)-2\varepsilon.

(118)

Upper bound on
L(f)
.

Let
g:=B\mathbf{1}-f\geq 0
. Apply (
118
) to
g
(with the same partition
P
):

L(g)\geq L(P;g,\alpha)-2\varepsilon.

But
L(g)=B\,L(\mathbf{1})-L(f)=B\alpha(1)-L(f)
and

L(P;g,\alpha)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\inf_{[t_{i-1},t_{i}]}(B-f)\,\bigl(\alpha(t_{i})-\alpha(t_{i-1})\bigr)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}(B-M_{i})\bigl(\alpha(t_{i})-\alpha(t_{i-1})\bigr)=B\alpha(1)-U(P;f,\alpha).

Hence

B\alpha(1)-L(f)\geq B\alpha(1)-U(P;f,\alpha)-2\varepsilon,

so

L(f)\leq U(P;f,\alpha)+2\varepsilon.

(119)

Let
I:=\int_{0}^{1}f\,d\alpha
(which exists by Step 5). Then for every partition,

L(P;f,\alpha)\leq I\leq U(P;f,\alpha).

With (
117
), we have
U(P;f,\alpha)-L(P;f,\alpha)<\varepsilon
, hence

I-\varepsilon<L(P;f,\alpha)\leq I\leq U(P;f,\alpha)<I+\varepsilon.

Combine with (
118
)–(
119
):

I-3\varepsilon<L(f)<I+3\varepsilon.

Let
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
to conclude

L(f)=\int_{0}^{1}f\,d\alpha\qquad\text{for all }f\in X,\ f\geq 0.

Now let’s deal with the case of arbitrary
f\in X
, denote
f=f^{+}-f^{-}
where

f^{+}(x):=\max\{f(x),0\},\qquad f^{-}(x):=\max\{-f(x),0\}.

Then
f^{\pm}\in X
,
f^{\pm}\geq 0
, and by Step 6,

L(f^{\pm})=\int_{0}^{1}f^{\pm}\,d\alpha.

By linearity of
L
and of the Riemann–Stieltjes integral on
X
,

L(f)=L(f^{+})-L(f^{-})=\int_{0}^{1}f^{+}\,d\alpha-\int_{0}^{1}f^{-}\,d\alpha=\int_{0}^{1}(f^{+}-f^{-})\,d\alpha=\int_{0}^{1}f\,d\alpha.

The Right–continuous modification is conducted because of the special need of the Main theorems of Lorenz Curve representation. Recall the definition of
\alpha^{\mathrm{rc}}
in (
12
).
Then
\alpha^{\mathrm{rc}}
is increasing. Moreover, for
t\in(0,1)
,

\lim_{h\downarrow 0}\alpha^{\mathrm{rc}}(t+h)=\lim_{h\downarrow 0}\alpha((t+h)+)=\alpha(t+)=\alpha^{\mathrm{rc}}(t),

so
\alpha^{\mathrm{rc}}
is right–continuous on
(0,1)
.

Fix
f\in X
. Since
\alpha^{\mathrm{rc}}
is increasing, Step 5 implies
\int_{0}^{1}f\,d\alpha^{\mathrm{rc}}
exists.
We show

\int_{0}^{1}f\,d\alpha^{\mathrm{rc}}=\int_{0}^{1}f\,d\alpha.

(120)

For a tagged partition
(P,\xi)
with
P:0=t_{0}<\cdots<t_{m}=1
and
\xi_{i}\in[t_{i-1},t_{i}]
, define

S(P,\xi;f,\beta):=\sum_{i=1}^{m}f(\xi_{i})\bigl(\beta(t_{i})-\beta(t_{i-1})\bigr),\qquad\beta\in\{\alpha,\alpha^{\mathrm{rc}}\}.

Set
\delta_{i}:=\alpha^{\mathrm{rc}}(t_{i})-\alpha(t_{i})
for
i=0,\dots,m
. Then
\delta_{0}=0
and
\delta_{m}=0
, and

\alpha^{\mathrm{rc}}(t_{i})-\alpha^{\mathrm{rc}}(t_{i-1})=\bigl(\alpha(t_{i})-\alpha(t_{i-1})\bigr)+(\delta_{i}-\delta_{i-1}).

Therefore

S(P,\xi;f,\alpha^{\mathrm{rc}})-S(P,\xi;f,\alpha)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}f(\xi_{i})(\delta_{i}-\delta_{i-1})=\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\bigl(f(\xi_{i})-f(\xi_{i+1})\bigr)\delta_{i},

since
\delta_{0}=\delta_{m}=0
.

Let
\|P\|:=\max_{1\leq i\leq m}(t_{i}-t_{i-1})
and let
\omega_{f}
be the modulus of continuity of
f
as in Step 5.
Because
\xi_{i}\in[t_{i-1},t_{i}]
and
\xi_{i+1}\in[t_{i},t_{i+1}]
, we have
|\xi_{i}-\xi_{i+1}|\leq t_{i+1}-t_{i-1}\leq 2\|P\|
, hence

|f(\xi_{i})-f(\xi_{i+1})|\leq\omega_{f}(2\|P\|).

Thus

\bigl|S(P,\xi;f,\alpha^{\mathrm{rc}})-S(P,\xi;f,\alpha)\bigr|\leq\omega_{f}(2\|P\|)\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\delta_{i}.

Now
\delta_{i}=\alpha(t_{i}+)-\alpha(t_{i})\geq 0
for
i=1,\dots,m-1
. For each such
i
, choose
\varepsilon_{i}>0
so small that the
intervals
[t_{i},t_{i}+\varepsilon_{i}]
are disjoint and contained in
[0,1]
. Then

\delta_{i}=\alpha(t_{i}+)-\alpha(t_{i})\leq\alpha(t_{i}+\varepsilon_{i})-\alpha(t_{i}).

Summing over
i=1,\dots,m-1
and using disjointness as in Claim 1 yields

\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\delta_{i}\leq\alpha(1)-\alpha(0).

Consequently,

\bigl|S(P,\xi;f,\alpha^{\mathrm{rc}})-S(P,\xi;f,\alpha)\bigr|\leq\omega_{f}(2\|P\|)\bigl(\alpha(1)-\alpha(0)\bigr).

Since
f
is uniformly continuous,
\omega_{f}(2\|P\|)\to 0
as
\|P\|\to 0
.
Therefore the difference between the Riemann–Stieltjes sums for
\alpha
and
\alpha^{\mathrm{rc}}
tends to
0
uniformly in the tags
as the mesh tends to
0
, which proves (
120
).

Finally, since
L(f)=\int_{0}^{1}f\,d\alpha
and (
120
) holds, we obtain

L(f)=\int_{0}^{1}f\,d\alpha^{\mathrm{rc}}\qquad(\forall f\in X).

∎

Proof A.9  (Proof of
Proposition

1
)

Back to
Proposition

1
.

Proof.

Write
L\succ M
for
L\succsim M
and
\neg(M\succsim L)
, and
L\sim M
for
L\succsim M
and
M\succsim L
.
Convexity of
\mathcal{L}
is immediate: if
L_{1},L_{2}\in\mathcal{L}
and
\alpha\in[0,1]
, then
(1-\alpha)L_{1}+\alpha L_{2}\in\mathcal{L}
.

We record the strict form of independence (used repeatedly): for all
L_{1},L_{2},L_{3}\in\mathcal{L}
and
\alpha\in(0,1)
,

L_{1}\succ L_{2}\iff(1-\alpha)L_{1}+\alpha L_{3}\succ(1-\alpha)L_{2}+\alpha L_{3}.

(121)

Proof: if
L_{1}\succ L_{2}
, then
L_{1}\succsim L_{2}
and
\neg(L_{2}\succsim L_{1})
; by Strong Independence,

(1-\alpha)L_{1}+\alpha L_{3}\succsim(1-\alpha)L_{2}+\alpha L_{3}
, and if also

(1-\alpha)L_{2}+\alpha L_{3}\succsim(1-\alpha)L_{1}+\alpha L_{3}
, then Strong Independence (reverse direction) gives

L_{2}\succsim L_{1}
, contradiction; hence the mixture is strict. Conversely, if the mixture is strict, then it is weak, hence

L_{1}\succsim L_{2}
by the reverse direction; if
L_{2}\succsim L_{1}
then the reverse mixture weak preference holds by Strong
Independence, contradicting strictness.

Fix
L^{1},L^{0}\in\mathcal{L}
with
L^{1}\succ L^{0}
(Axiom
4
).

Fix
X,Y\in\mathcal{L}
with
X\succ Y
and write
m(\alpha):=\alpha X+(1-\alpha)Y
for
\alpha\in[0,1]
.
If
0\leq\beta<\alpha\leq 1
, set
\lambda:=\frac{\alpha-\beta}{1-\beta}\in(0,1]
and compute

\lambda X+(1-\lambda)m(\beta)=\lambda X+(1-\lambda)\bigl(\beta X+(1-\beta)Y\bigr)=\bigl(\beta+\lambda(1-\beta)\bigr)X+\bigl(1-\beta-\lambda(1-\beta)\bigr)Y=\alpha X+(1-\alpha)Y=m(\alpha).

Since
X\succ Y
, applying (
121
) with
(L_{1},L_{2},L_{3})=(X,Y,X)
and weight
\beta\in(0,1)
gives

X=(1-\beta)X+\beta X\succ(1-\beta)Y+\beta X=m(\beta)
, and then applying (
121
) with

(L_{1},L_{2},L_{3})=(X,m(\beta),m(\beta))
and weight
\lambda
yields
m(\alpha)=\lambda X+(1-\lambda)m(\beta)\succ m(\beta)
.
Thus

0\leq\beta<\alpha\leq 1\quad\Longrightarrow\quad m(\alpha)\succ m(\beta).

(122)

Define the interval
[Y,X]:=\{L\in\mathcal{L}:\ X\succsim L\succsim Y\}
.
If
L_{1},L_{2}\in[Y,X]
and
\theta\in[0,1]
, then
X\succsim L_{i}
and
L_{i}\succsim Y
for
i=1,2
, and Strong Independence gives

X=(1-\theta)X+\theta X\succsim(1-\theta)L_{1}+\theta X,\qquad(1-\theta)L_{1}+\theta X\succsim(1-\theta)L_{1}+\theta L_{2},

hence
X\succsim(1-\theta)L_{1}+\theta L_{2}
by transitivity; similarly,

(1-\theta)L_{1}+\theta L_{2}\succsim(1-\theta)Y+\theta L_{2},\qquad(1-\theta)Y+\theta L_{2}\succsim(1-\theta)Y+\theta Y=Y,

hence
(1-\theta)L_{1}+\theta L_{2}\succsim Y
. Therefore
[Y,X]
is convex.

Now fix
L\in[Y,X]
and set

A_{L}:=\{\alpha\in[0,1]:\ m(\alpha)\succsim L\},\qquad B_{L}:=\{\alpha\in[0,1]:\ L\succsim m(\alpha)\}.

By completeness,
A_{L}\cup B_{L}=[0,1]
, and
1\in A_{L}
(since
X\succsim L
) and
0\in B_{L}
(since
L\succsim Y
),
so both sets are nonempty. If
\alpha_{n}\in A_{L}
and
\alpha_{n}\to\alpha
, then
m(\alpha_{n})\succsim L
for all
n
; since

\alpha\mapsto m(\alpha)
is continuous in
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
and
\operatorname{graph}(\succsim)
is closed
(Axiom
3
), we get
m(\alpha)\succsim L
, hence
\alpha\in A_{L}
; thus
A_{L}
is closed, and similarly
B_{L}
is closed.
As
[0,1]
is connected and
A_{L},B_{L}
are nonempty closed with union
[0,1]
, we have
A_{L}\cap B_{L}\neq\varnothing
; pick

\alpha_{L}\in A_{L}\cap B_{L}
, so
m(\alpha_{L})\succsim L\succsim m(\alpha_{L})
, i.e.

L\sim m(\alpha_{L}).

(123)

If also
L\sim m(\beta)
, then either
\beta=\alpha_{L}
or, say,
\beta<\alpha_{L}
, and (
122
) gives

m(\alpha_{L})\succ m(\beta)
, contradicting transitivity with
L\sim m(\alpha_{L})
and
L\sim m(\beta)
; hence
\alpha_{L}
is unique.
Define
u_{X,Y}:[Y,X]\to[0,1]
by
u_{X,Y}(L):=\alpha_{L}
.

If
L,M\in[Y,X]
and
u_{X,Y}(L)=\alpha
,
u_{X,Y}(M)=\beta
, then
L\sim m(\alpha)
and
M\sim m(\beta)
by (
123
).
By (
122
) and completeness,
m(\alpha)\succsim m(\beta)
iff
\alpha\geq\beta
; therefore, by transitivity,

L\succsim M\iff u_{X,Y}(L)\geq u_{X,Y}(M)\qquad(L,M\in[Y,X]).

(124)

Moreover, if
L,M\in[Y,X]
and
\theta\in[0,1]
, then convexity of
[Y,X]
gives
N:=(1-\theta)L+\theta M\in[Y,X]
.
Let
\alpha:=u_{X,Y}(L)
and
\beta:=u_{X,Y}(M)
, so
L\sim m(\alpha)
and
M\sim m(\beta)
. Strong Independence implies

(1-\theta)L+\theta M\sim(1-\theta)m(\alpha)+\theta M\sim(1-\theta)m(\alpha)+\theta m(\beta)=\bigl((1-\theta)\alpha+\theta\beta\bigr)X+\Bigl(1-\bigl((1-\theta)\alpha+\theta\beta\bigr)\Bigr)Y=m\bigl((1-\theta)\alpha+\theta\beta\bigr),

and by uniqueness in (
123
),

u_{X,Y}\bigl((1-\theta)L+\theta M\bigr)=(1-\theta)u_{X,Y}(L)+\theta u_{X,Y}(M).

(125)

Now assume
X,Y\in\mathcal{L}
satisfy
X\succsim L^{1}\succ L^{0}\succsim Y
, so
L^{1},L^{0}\in[Y,X]
and
u_{X,Y}
is defined there.
Define on
[Y,X]
the normalized affine map

U_{X,Y}(L):=\frac{u_{X,Y}(L)-u_{X,Y}(L^{0})}{u_{X,Y}(L^{1})-u_{X,Y}(L^{0})}.

Since
u_{X,Y}
represents
\succsim
on
[Y,X]
by (
124
) and
L^{1}\succ L^{0}
, we have

u_{X,Y}(L^{1})>u_{X,Y}(L^{0})
, so
U_{X,Y}
is well-defined, represents
\succsim
on
[Y,X]
, is affine on
[Y,X]

(by (
125
)), and satisfies
U_{X,Y}(L^{1})=1
,
U_{X,Y}(L^{0})=0
.

For each
L\in\mathcal{L}
, choose some
X,Y\in\mathcal{L}
with
X\succsim L^{1}\succ L^{0}\succsim Y
and
L\in[Y,X]

(which is possible by completeness: if
L\succsim L^{1}
take
(X,Y)=(L,L^{0})
; if
L^{1}\succsim L\succsim L^{0}
take

(X,Y)=(L^{1},L^{0})
; if
L^{0}\succsim L
take
(X,Y)=(L^{1},L)
), and define
U(L):=U_{X,Y}(L)
.
To see this does not depend on the choice, fix any admissible
(X,Y)
with
L^{1},L^{0},L\in[Y,X]
.
If
L^{1}\succsim L\succsim L^{0}
, then by the construction applied on
[L^{0},L^{1}]
there is a unique
\alpha\in[0,1]
such that

L\sim\alpha L^{1}+(1-\alpha)L^{0}
, hence, by affinity and normalization of
U_{X,Y}
,

U_{X,Y}(L)=U_{X,Y}(\alpha L^{1}+(1-\alpha)L^{0})=\alpha.

If
L\succsim L^{1}
, then
L\succ L^{0}
, and applying the same construction on
[L^{0},L]
yields a unique
\alpha\in(0,1]
such that

L^{1}\sim\alpha L+(1-\alpha)L^{0}
, hence

1=U_{X,Y}(L^{1})=U_{X,Y}(\alpha L+(1-\alpha)L^{0})=\alpha U_{X,Y}(L)+(1-\alpha)U_{X,Y}(L^{0})=\alpha U_{X,Y}(L),

so
U_{X,Y}(L)=1/\alpha
. If
L^{0}\succsim L
, then
L^{1}\succ L
, and applying the construction on
[L,L^{1}]
yields a unique

\alpha\in(0,1)
such that
L^{0}\sim\alpha L^{1}+(1-\alpha)L
, hence

0=U_{X,Y}(L^{0})=U_{X,Y}(\alpha L^{1}+(1-\alpha)L)=\alpha U_{X,Y}(L^{1})+(1-\alpha)U_{X,Y}(L)=\alpha+(1-\alpha)U_{X,Y}(L),

so
U_{X,Y}(L)=-\alpha/(1-\alpha)
. In each case, the computed value depends only on
L
and the fixed pair
L^{1}\succ L^{0}
,
so
U
is well-defined on
\mathcal{L}
and satisfies
U(L^{1})=1
,
U(L^{0})=0
, hence is nonconstant.

For (i), given
L_{1},L_{2}\in\mathcal{L}
, let
X
be a
\succsim
-maximal element of
\{L^{1},L_{1},L_{2}\}
and

Y
a
\succsim
-minimal element of
\{L^{0},L_{1},L_{2}\}
(exist by completeness). Then
X\succsim L^{1}\succ L^{0}\succsim Y

and
L_{1},L_{2}\in[Y,X]
, so
U
coincides with
U_{X,Y}
on
[Y,X]
and
U_{X,Y}
represents
\succsim
there; thus

L_{1}\succsim L_{2}\iff U(L_{1})\geq U(L_{2})
.

For (ii), fix
L_{1},L_{2}\in\mathcal{L}
and
\theta\in[0,1]
and use the same
X,Y
as above; since
[Y,X]
is convex,

(1-\theta)L_{1}+\theta L_{2}\in[Y,X]
, and
U=U_{X,Y}
is affine on
[Y,X]
, so

U((1-\theta)L_{1}+\theta L_{2})=(1-\theta)U(L_{1})+\theta U(L_{2})
.

For (iii), let
L_{n}\to L
in
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
and suppose
U(L_{n})\not\to U(L)
. Then there exist
\varepsilon>0
and a subsequence
(not relabeled) such that either
U(L_{n})\geq U(L)+\varepsilon
for all
n
or
U(L_{n})\leq U(L)-\varepsilon
for all
n
; assume the first.
Choose
n_{0}
and set
H:=L_{n_{0}}
, so
U(H)\geq U(L)+\varepsilon
and
U(H)>U(L)
. Let

t:=\frac{\varepsilon/2}{U(H)-U(L)}\in(0,1),\qquad L^{\sharp}:=(1-t)L+tH\in\mathcal{L},

so by affinity
U(L^{\sharp})=(1-t)U(L)+tU(H)=U(L)+\varepsilon/2
. Then for all
n
,

U(L_{n})\geq U(L)+\varepsilon>U(L^{\sharp})
, hence
L_{n}\succ L^{\sharp}
, so
(L_{n},L^{\sharp})\in\operatorname{graph}(\succsim)
.
By closedness of
\operatorname{graph}(\succsim)
and
L_{n}\to L
, we get
(L,L^{\sharp})\in\operatorname{graph}(\succsim)
, i.e.
L\succsim L^{\sharp}
,
hence
U(L)\geq U(L^{\sharp})
by (i), contradicting
U(L^{\sharp})=U(L)+\varepsilon/2
. Thus
U(L_{n})\to U(L)
.

For (iv), let
V:\mathcal{L}\to\mathbb{R}
also satisfy (i)–(ii). Set
a:=V(L^{1})-V(L^{0})
and
b:=V(L^{0})
; since
L^{1}\succ L^{0}
and
V
represents

\succsim
, we have
a>0
. Fix
L\in\mathcal{L}
. If
L^{1}\succsim L\succsim L^{0}
, let
\alpha\in[0,1]
be the unique number with

L\sim\alpha L^{1}+(1-\alpha)L^{0}
; then by affinity,

V(L)=\alpha V(L^{1})+(1-\alpha)V(L^{0})=a\alpha+b
, while by definition
U(L)=\alpha
, so
V(L)=aU(L)+b
.
If
L\succsim L^{1}
, let
\alpha\in(0,1]
be the unique number with
L^{1}\sim\alpha L+(1-\alpha)L^{0}
; then

V(L^{1})=\alpha V(L)+(1-\alpha)V(L^{0})
gives
V(L)=a(1/\alpha)+b
, while
U(L)=1/\alpha
, so again
V(L)=aU(L)+b
.
If
L^{0}\succsim L
, let
\alpha\in(0,1)
be the unique number with
L^{0}\sim\alpha L^{1}+(1-\alpha)L
; then

V(L^{0})=\alpha V(L^{1})+(1-\alpha)V(L)
gives
V(L)=b-a\,\alpha/(1-\alpha)
, while
U(L)=-\alpha/(1-\alpha)
, so
V(L)=aU(L)+b
.
Hence
V=aU+b
on
\mathcal{L}
, and the normalization
U(L^{1})=1
,
U(L^{0})=0
is already in force.∎

Proof A.10  (Proof of
Lemma

9
)

Back to
Lemma

9
.

Proof.

Define

F(n):=\log U(n)\qquad(n\in\mathbb{N}).

Then
F:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies:

Step 1:
F
is completely additive.
For all
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
,

F(mn)=\log U(mn)=\log\bigl(U(m)U(n)\bigr)=\log U(m)+\log U(n)=F(m)+F(n).

Also
F(1)=\log U(1)=\log(U(1\cdot 1))=\log(U(1)^{2})
forces
U(1)=1
, hence
F(1)=0
.

Step 2:
\Delta F(n)\to 0
.
Let
\Delta F(n):=F(n+1)-F(n)
. Then

\Delta F(n)=\log U(n+1)-\log U(n)=\log\!\left(\frac{U(n+1)}{U(n)}\right).

Since
\frac{U(n+1)}{U(n)}\to 1
by (ii) and
\log
is continuous at
1
, we have

\Delta F(n)\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}0.

Step 3: g-adic contraction.
Fix an integer
g\geq 2
. For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, define a finite chain

n_{0}:=n,\qquad n_{i}:=\left\lfloor\frac{n_{i-1}}{g}\right\rfloor\quad(i\geq 1),

and remainders

r_{i}:=n_{i-1}-gn_{i}\in\{0,1,\dots,g-1\}.

Let
k=k_{g}(n)
be the first index such that
n_{k}<g
(so
n_{k}\in\{1,2,\dots,g-1\}
).

Step 3a:
g^{k}\leq n<g^{k+1}
.
From
n_{i-1}=gn_{i}+r_{i}\geq gn_{i}
we get
n_{i}\leq n_{i-1}/g
, hence
n_{k}\leq n/g^{k}
. Since
n_{k}\geq 1
,

n\geq g^{k}.

Also
n_{i-1}=gn_{i}+r_{i}\leq gn_{i}+(g-1)<g(n_{i}+1)
. Iterating,

n=n_{0}<g^{k}(n_{k}+1)\leq g^{k}\cdot g=g^{k+1}.

Thus
g^{k}\leq n<g^{k+1}
.

Step 3b: one-step approximation.
Let
\varepsilon>0
. Since
\Delta F(n)\to 0
, there exists
N=N(\varepsilon,g)\in\mathbb{N}
such that

m\geq N\quad\Longrightarrow\quad|\Delta F(m)|=|F(m+1)-F(m)|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{g}.

Fix an index
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
with
gn_{i}\geq N
. Then

n_{i-1}=gn_{i}+r_{i}.

Using complete additivity,

F(gn_{i})=F(g)+F(n_{i}).

Hence

F(n_{i-1})-F(n_{i})-F(g)=F(gn_{i}+r_{i})-F(gn_{i}).

If
r_{i}=0
this is
0
. If
r_{i}\geq 1
, then

F(gn_{i}+r_{i})-F(gn_{i})=\sum_{j=0}^{r_{i}-1}\Bigl(F(gn_{i}+j+1)-F(gn_{i}+j)\Bigr).

For each
0\leq j\leq r_{i}-1
we have
gn_{i}+j\geq gn_{i}\geq N
, so by (
\ast
),

\bigl|F(gn_{i}+j+1)-F(gn_{i}+j)\bigr|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{g}.

Therefore

\bigl|F(n_{i-1})-F(n_{i})-F(g)\bigr|\leq r_{i}\cdot\frac{\varepsilon}{g}\leq(g-1)\cdot\frac{\varepsilon}{g}<\varepsilon.

Step 4: summing along the chain.
Write the telescoping identity

F(n)=F(n_{0})=F(n_{k})+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\bigl(F(n_{i-1})-F(n_{i})\bigr)=F(n_{k})+kF(g)+\sum_{i=1}^{k}e_{i},

where

e_{i}:=F(n_{i-1})-F(n_{i})-F(g).

Split
\{1,\dots,k\}=I_{\mathrm{big}}\cup I_{\mathrm{small}}
where

I_{\mathrm{big}}:=\{\,i:\ gn_{i}\geq N\,\},\qquad I_{\mathrm{small}}:=\{1,\dots,k\}\setminus I_{\mathrm{big}}.

For
i\in I_{\mathrm{big}}
,
(\dagger)
gives
|e_{i}|<\varepsilon
, hence

\sum_{i\in I_{\mathrm{big}}}|e_{i}|\leq\varepsilon\,|I_{\mathrm{big}}|\leq\varepsilon k.

For
i\in I_{\mathrm{small}}
we have
gn_{i}<N
, hence
n_{i}\leq N-1
. Consequently,

n_{i-1}=gn_{i}+r_{i}\leq g(N-1)+(g-1)=gN-1.

Thus, for
i\in I_{\mathrm{small}}
, both
(n_{i-1},n_{i})
lie in the finite set

\{1,2,\dots,gN-1\}\times\{1,2,\dots,N-1\}.

Define the finite constant

B:=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq a\leq gN-1\\
1\leq b\leq N-1\end{subarray}}\bigl|F(a)-F(b)-F(g)\bigr|<\infty.

Then
|e_{i}|\leq B
for all
i\in I_{\mathrm{small}}
. Also, since
n_{i}
decreases by a factor
\geq g
each step until it falls below
N
, the number
|I_{\mathrm{small}}|
is bounded by a constant depending only on
(N,g)
; one crude bound is

|I_{\mathrm{small}}|\leq 1+\left\lceil\log_{g}N\right\rceil=:M.

Hence

\sum_{i\in I_{\mathrm{small}}}|e_{i}|\leq BM.

Moreover,
n_{k}\in\{1,\dots,g-1\}
, so

|F(n_{k})|\leq\max_{1\leq t\leq g-1}|F(t)|=:C_{0}<\infty.

Combining (1)–(3),

|F(n)-kF(g)|\leq|F(n_{k})|+\sum_{i=1}^{k}|e_{i}|\leq C_{0}+\varepsilon k+BM.

Thus, for each fixed
g\geq 2
and each
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a constant
C=C(\varepsilon,g)
such that

|F(n)-k_{g}(n)F(g)|\leq\varepsilon\,k_{g}(n)+C\qquad\forall n\in\mathbb{N}.

Step 5: identify the logarithmic slope.
From
g^{k}\leq n<g^{k+1}
we have

k\log g\leq\log n<(k+1)\log g\quad\Longrightarrow\quad 0\leq\frac{\log n}{\log g}-k<1.

Divide (4) by
\log n
and use
k\leq\frac{\log n}{\log g}
from (5):

\left|\frac{F(n)}{\log n}-\frac{kF(g)}{\log n}\right|\leq\varepsilon\,\frac{k}{\log n}+\frac{C}{\log n}\leq\varepsilon\cdot\frac{1}{\log g}+\frac{C}{\log n}.

Also, from (5),

\left|\frac{k}{\log n}-\frac{1}{\log g}\right|=\frac{1}{\log n}\left|\frac{\log n}{\log g}-k\right|\leq\frac{1}{\log n}.

Therefore

\left|\frac{kF(g)}{\log n}-\frac{F(g)}{\log g}\right|\leq|F(g)|\cdot\left|\frac{k}{\log n}-\frac{1}{\log g}\right|\leq\frac{|F(g)|}{\log n}.

Combine (6) and (7):

\left|\frac{F(n)}{\log n}-\frac{F(g)}{\log g}\right|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{\log g}+\frac{C+|F(g)|}{\log n}.

Let
n\to\infty
(with
g,\varepsilon
fixed):

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\left|\frac{F(n)}{\log n}-\frac{F(g)}{\log g}\right|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{\log g}.

Since
\varepsilon>0
is arbitrary,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{F(n)}{\log n}=\frac{F(g)}{\log g}.

Step 6: independence of
g
and exact identification of
F
.
Equation (8) holds for every integer
g\geq 2
. In particular, for
g=2
and any
g\geq 2
,

\frac{F(g)}{\log g}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{F(n)}{\log n}=\frac{F(2)}{\log 2}.

Define

r:=\frac{F(2)}{\log 2}.

Then for every
g\geq 2
,

F(g)=r\log g.

Since
g
here is an arbitrary integer
\geq 2
, this gives

F(n)=r\log n\qquad\forall n\geq 2,

and also
F(1)=0=r\log 1
.

Step 7: return to
U
.
For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

U(n)=\exp(F(n))=\exp(r\log n)=n^{\,r}.

This proves the lemma.∎

*
